# Continuity State

## How to read this file, and why the guide is at the top

**This file is a rolling window. It holds the global canon, the whole of the volume currently being written, and nothing older, and everything older is in `state/archive/continuity-volumes-01-12.md`, verbatim, with every item number exactly as it was here.** The window moved for Volumes 01 to 07 at the Volume 07 close and for Volumes 01 to 08 at the Volume 08 close, **and after that cut this file holds the global canon and nothing else, because Volume 09 has not been written yet.** The reason is arithmetic and it is not a preference. As of the review of Batch 0004 this file was **1.94 MB and about 373,000 words, growing about 1,100 words a chapter, which is roughly a third of the manuscript**, and it was being carried whole into every phase, which is the opposite of what AGENTS.md asks a rolling summary to be. It is now about 170,000 characters, and it stays about that size for as long as the window is maintained.

**Three rules make this safe, and a phase that breaks one of them has broken canon rather than tidied it.**

1. **Items are never renumbered.** 1 to 1000 are fixed and are what they were, and the highest of them, item 1000, is the last item of the Volume 08 close. 646 onward were appended by volume, by batch and by repair pass in the order they were established, and that is unchanged. **An item number means the same thing whether you find it here or in the archive**, and the archive carries the same numbers, so *item 412* is findable either way.
2. **Nothing is deleted, only moved.** The archive is a byte-for-byte copy of what was cut, and the four files under `state/archive/` reconstruct any earlier state of any of the four ledgers exactly.
3. **A volume is never archived while it is being read.** The window moves forward at a volume close, after the close has audited the volume, and not before.

**Read this guide, then read the section for the volume you are actually working on, and do not read the whole thing. For the Volume 09 first prompt the useful reading is: this guide, `state/current.md`, `outline/volumes/volume-08-close.md` §§5, 6, 7, 9 and 10, the seventeen threads under *Threads the Volume 08 close hands to Volume 09* in `state/archive/continuity-volumes-01-12.md`, and items 984 to 1000 in `state/archive/continuity-volumes-01-12.md`. Everything before item 984 is in the archive and is reachable by grep, not by reading.** **The highest item at any time is the newest, and the number is in `state/current.md`.**

**The archive index, so a number can be found without grepping a megabyte.** `state/archive/continuity-volumes-01-12.md` holds, in order and unaltered, everything `continuity-volumes-01-07.md` holds plus all of Volume 08 and the Volume 08 close: the Volume 01 to Volume 05 batch and close sections, the six Volume 06 sections, the Volume 06 close, the Volume 07 batch sections and its close, the five Volume 08 batch sections, their review-repair sections, and the Volume 08 close's own items 984 to 1000. **Items 1 to 645 are Volume 01 to Volume 05 and are fixed. Items 646 to 897 are Volume 06, Volume 07's batches and the Volume 07 close. Items 898 to 983 are the Volume 07 close's tail and the five Volume 08 batch sections. Items 984 to 1000 are the Volume 08 close. The live window is empty and opens on Volume 09.**

**Items that are marked superseded in part are the point of them, and the markers are the point.** Item 746 is a block device ledger whose seven figures did not reproduce on their own stated basis, and item 748 reported a sweep of a block clean when three of the paragraphs it claimed to have repaired were still on the page. Both are left standing as the first printing and both point forward. A writer who needs a Volume 06 Block 4 figure takes it from item 757, and a writer who needs to know what the block believed takes it from 746 and 748 and knows that nine figures in them were wrong.

**One warning that cost a real error. Do not trust a line-number pointer into a chapter file in this ledger.** Every repair pass inserts and removes lines, so a pointer written by one phase is stale by the next one. Item 697 pointed the four lines of the winter book at `chapters/volume-06/chapter-0266.md:39` and the actual positions were 47; after the Batch 0002 review repair they are 45, 47, 49 and 51. **Refer to a passage by its chapter and its opening words instead, and if you must use a line number, re-derive it in the same turn you use it.**

Bootstrap canon is established in `bible/`, `outline/series.md`, and `outline/ending.md`. **Volume 08 is complete and closed; the close record is `outline/volumes/volume-08-close.md` and it is the authority for items 984 to 1000 and for every figure in this project's current state.**

## Hard canon

- World: Neyra, a late-medieval river-and-road world. Years count from the **Quiet Reckoning**; the story opens in **YR 312, Mudmonth**.
- Protagonist: Sir Aren Kest, thirty-two, a former Lantern Guard captain and current bridge repairer in Merefen. Estranged from Tovan; still idealizes Edric.
- Starting mystery: the Bellweather Sundering, **YR 301, Mudmonth**, erased Bellweather and seven villages. The story opens on the eleventh anniversary, in the same season. The Crown blames Aren’s failed bridge defense.
- The seven villages: Alder Cross, Saltbrim, Wrenlow, Cobbett, Nine Elms, Stonebarrow, Hushwater.
- Deeper answer: Marshal Edric Hunn and the Lantern Council ordered a forced cull to contain the Unwritten Wound; the order sacrificed a living city.
- Gatehouse rule: a living path cannot be reset or forcibly closed while conscious life exists inside it. Closing a route removes Aren’s access, not the world.
- **Local custody rule (added in bootstrap):** roads are opened by named local custodians, not by Aren. Two consenting custodians with paired anchors can open a road without him, and path communities can open roads to each other without the main world. Aren is a translator and temporary common anchor, never a necessary mechanism. The ending must show roads working without him.
- Path capacity: one at Stage 0/First Stitch, then two, three, five, seven, and ten at later stages. The final Unowned Road stage removes exclusive personal access.
- Path costs: roadmarks, memory loss, path bleed, lost time, failed workings, social obligations, and permanent local consequences. No path is a disposable map.
- Crossing budget: about three attempted crossings per season at any stage; **three days of fever after a return, at Stage 0 and at Stage 1 alike** — the first crossing in Volume 01 pays the same three days — growing to a month at Stage 6. A return stitch is consumed by use.
- Final enemy: Edric Hunn, using the established First Witness protocol to activate the One Road. No new final enemy is planned.

## Ending constraints

The ending must preserve Bellweather-Living as a living sovereign community, contain but not magically cure the Unwritten Wound, ratify the Charter of Many Roads, keep Aren and Mara’s relationship voluntary and independent, and leave Edric alive for public judgment.

## Knowledge control

`outline/series.md` contains a per-volume table of who knows what. Do not reveal a fact to any character before the volume listed there. This is the primary tool for protecting the central mystery across 900 chapters.

## Canon clarifications recorded in this phase

1. **Name change.** The Culling Order field warden was renamed **Sera Dain → Sabin Dain** to remove a collision with Sera Quen. Use Sabin Dain in all prose and state.
2. **Name change.** The Lantern Council chancellor was renamed **Veyra Senn → Iselde Senn** to remove a collision with Mara Vey.
3. **Cast discipline.** At most six new named characters per batch. Every recurring character needs one want unrelated to Aren. No supporting character is killed for shock.
4. **The bell has provenance.** Crown salvage, sold at a Merefen clearance for two pence, bought by Aren as scrap bronze in the opening Mudmonth. It is a damaged anchor, not a key or a summons.
5. **Mara’s archive is the main-world Bellweather Claims Archive** in Merefen’s salt-loft rooms. It is not the living archive inside Bellweather-Living, which is run by Hollis Dree and refuses to surrender copies.
6. **Relationship policy is decided:** one slow-burn relationship, Aren and Mara Vey. No harem.
7. **Aren’s age arithmetic.** Aren is 32 at the opening and was **21** at the Sundering (born YR 280, apprenticed at twelve, Guard at seventeen, commissioned by brevet at twenty-one). This is deliberate: he was the youngest captain in the Guard, which is why Edric chose him, why the Crown can blame a boy, and why Aren believed his own defence for three years. Do not rewrite the Sundering as a child being made to do it alone. He held a real command and really failed it.
8. **The Rusk families are separated.** Jos Rusk, the Hushwater ferryman, survives and lives in Merefen; his claim is the first one Mara Vey personally refutes, and he is wrong about the cause. His wife **Anwen Rusk** (37, who kept the Hushwater ferry’s crossing book) and daughter **Bryn Rusk** (11) are among the erased and are now named outright instead of being left as “the wife and daughter.” The recurring Merefen ferryman formerly called Pei Rusk is **Pei Tarrow** (52, Hushwater survivor’s son, freight boats on the Old Toll channel). He is no relation to Jos Rusk. Do not stage the two men together, and do not let a bystander call Pei a Rusk.
9. **Hanna Vey and Mara Vey are not related.** The shared surname is deliberate common naming from the Sable river country, not a plot relation, not a mistake, and not a reason to rename either of them later. Let Mara notice the coincidence once, early and coldly, and never return to it. No scene may imply kinship.
10. **Toll figures are locked.** Bellweather city: about **four thousand**. The seven villages: about **seven hundred and twenty** (Alder Cross 200, Saltbrim 90, Wrenlow 60, Cobbett 140, Nine Elms 110, Stonebarrow 40, Hushwater 80). The Crown quotes the city figure alone and folds the villages into it; the Archive keeps the two numbers apart on every page and never prints a combined total. In a book whose moral weight rests on exact arithmetic, a character arguing about the dead must use the number actually being argued about.
11. **Dispatch reality, for the record.** The runner selects the first `workspace/**/PROMPT.md` in sorted order that has no `.done` or `.blocked` marker. `state/phase-ledger.json` and `PHASE_SYSTEM.md` are controller-owned, are never written by the writer, and the ledger is not what dispatch reads — so the only protection against a repeated batch is the prompt files themselves. Consequence: **exactly one prompt may exist per batch.** The duplicate `workspace/phase-002-batch-plan/` prompt, which also asked for Chapters 1–10, was deleted during the phase-000 fix pass. `workspace/volume-01/batch-0001/PROMPT.md` is the single prompt for the first batch and pairs with `outline/batches/volume-01-batch-0001.md`. The `.wip-conflict` marker in `workspace/phase-000-bootstrap/` only gates WIP-branch resume and is cleared by the runner on completion; leave it alone.
12. **The named dead are records, not cast.** Ferris Oat, Hanna Vey, Corin Slate, Bess Alder, Anwen Rusk, and Bryn Rusk are read aloud, quoted, filed, and argued over; they do not act on the page and do not count against the six-new-named-characters-per-batch limit. That limit applies to characters who speak, decide, or obstruct. A survivor or claimant who acts is a character and does count: Jos Rusk and Pei Tarrow are both cast, not records.

## Canon clarifications recorded in the Volume 01 outline phase

13. **The Sundering's date is the nineteenth of Mudmonth.** The story opens on the sixth of Mudmonth, YR 312, so the eleventh anniversary falls thirteen days later, and the storm comes up on the night of the eighteenth into the nineteenth. Volume 01 opens on the sixth of Mudmonth and closes in the last week of Fallowmonth, about five months of main-world time, matching the series time ledger.
14. **Aren's aunt is dead.** She raised him after Ilyra's crossing and died of the low-quarter fever three winters before the opening, so Aren has no household, rents one room above a cooper on Weir Street, and nobody at home has to be told where he is. The person who holds his tools and covers for him is Tovan.
15. **The bell's economics and dates are fixed.** Aren buys the bell on the first of Mudmonth, YR 312, at the Crown clearance for two pence, **five days** before Chapter 1 opens on the sixth. Chapter 2 is the **eleventh**, the day the Merefen bridge guild's removal notice reaches him, so the notice is dated the day it arrives — eight days before the anniversary, and before any storm. The guild's standing valuation is **six marks** as scrap bronze, because the crack is worth the difference and the roll is right. In Chapter 2 Aren cannot raise six marks and signs a guild bond for the sum with his tool-chest as surety. He refuses Tovan's honest six-mark offer and takes the bond instead, on purpose: the metal buys nothing, a bell in a yard belongs to somebody else, and a bond puts the bell in his name in front of the guild. He has chosen to be the man answerable for it, and he never says so to anyone. The hold he signs in Chapter 1 is good until the guild's next sitting, **the twenty-fourth** — and nothing happens on the twenty-fourth, because a bench cannot act against a man who is not in front of it and Aren is nine days gone. That is the town's own process for a cracked bell, and the watch's sealed order replaces it three days later, on the twenty-seventh, without anybody in Merefen getting to choose between them. The bond lapses when the watch takes the bell, which is a smaller humiliation than the market days the closed road costs.
16. **Return stitches cannot be improvised for free.** Aren rings the bell without having prepared a return stitch, which is the mistake that shapes the batch: he is stranded for six path days, loses **nine** days of the main-world calendar against a Gatehouse warning of three, and returns on the twenty-seventh to a bell already seized. A working time-diverge warning in this series is wrong by days or weeks, never silently by months, and the drift is stated in the prose as a warning that was wrong.
17. **The path's own answer comes from two institutions.** The Bellweather Bridge Guild kept the **counterfoil** — the stub proving a severance writ was sent to Bellweather-Living on the nineteenth of Mudmonth, YR 301, refused on the deck of the arch and withdrawn. The **outgoing writ register** naming the signatory is kept by the toll houses, who are the guild's political opponents. The volume midpoint reveal lives in the toll-house counting room, not the guild hall.
18. **Path-side vocabulary is the volume's argument.** Bellweather-Living maintains its crossing through **stewards**; a steward maintains. The corrupted Crown term is **private keeper**, and a provincial document applies it to Aren during Batch 0001. The legitimate word *custodian* is not used in Volume 01. The word "keeper" fitting Aren is a Volume 01–03 hook and is never explained in Volume 01.
19. **Batch 0001's six names are fixed:** Tovan Kest, Mara Vey, Wenna Koll, Lysa Fenn, Captain Rell, Sera Quen — all six already established in `bible/characters.md` with a want, a flaw, and a function, and named in prose for the first time in this batch. Jos Rusk, Bram Ottery, Halven, Pei Tarrow, Sarn Oyelaran, and Ilvane Quarren are Batch 0002. Bram Ottery therefore **does not carry out the bell seizure in Chapters 8–10**: the seizure is Rell's watch, and its sergeant is addressed by office and left unnamed so the six-name cap is not spent on a man who performs one errand. Bram enters by name in Volume 02, when somebody has to answer in writing. Rell's unlawful seizure remains the fact recorded against him. A full batch-by-batch cast plan is in `outline/volume-01.md`.
20. **Batch 0001 uses two POVs plus one.** Aren in eight chapters, Mara in Chapter 3, Tovan in Chapter 9. The path-side institutions in the batch are addressed by office — the bench, the bench-clerk, the hull-master, the toll-house steward — so the six-name cap is not spent on people who only exist to be quoted.
21. **Two Gatehouse lines in the whole first batch,** one at the ringing and one at the return, both plain and both technically true in a way that conceals the moral cost.
22. **Planted in Batch 0001, owed later.** The bell bought for two pence (Chapter 1); the Merefen bridge guild's removal notice **dated the eleventh, the day it arrives** — before the storm and before any crossing (Chapter 2), which pays off when the watch's sealed seizure order turns out to be dated the same week (Chapter 8); Ferris Oat's brine weights, **held and cited but not filed** — the extract Mara trades for his promise (Chapter 3) — with the formal entry of the named roll of the dead reserved for **Chapter 892**, where it is the first document the Charter names; the bell ringing in the anniversary storm (Chapter 4); the empty iron cradle left on the near abutment after the seizure (Chapter 8), which is **not** still empty in Chapter 50 — the town reclaims its own iron during the Block 5 resolution, bands the crack, and the bell returns to the bridge without ever returning to Aren; Aren's deferred field notes and his three years of repeating the official fever account, disclosed in his own hand in Chapter 10, together with the two omissions he files alongside them. These are the Volume 01 setups named in `outline/ending.md`, and the writer must not add a second version of any of them. In particular, the brine weights are a cited authority in Volume 01 and a Chapter 892 payoff; filing them as an exhibit in Chapters 10 or 48 spends the ending's beat early and must not happen.
23. **Volume 01's cast discipline on the Culling Order.** No named member appears in Volume 01. Sabin Dain enters in Volume 2. The phrase "Culling Order" may appear at most once in Volume 01, in a document or a claimant's mouth, with no member attached to it.
24. **Hushwater Reach exists in the path.** Bellweather-Living has a living hamlet on a channel that is drowned in the main world, and its ferry cooperative keeps a crossing book in a living hand with different names in it. The cooperative counts **ferry hands and hearths, not souls**, so nobody can put a number on the hamlet; the best estimate in play is **near sixty**. That is deliberately far from the **eighty** erased at Hushwater in the locked toll figures, because a living hamlet of exactly eighty beside an erased village of exactly eighty is a signpost and this volume is only allowed a hint. No character notices the gap, and no one explains the coincidence in Volume 01. The thread stays held until Volume 09.
25. **The first crossing's calendar is locked, and so is the fever.** Crossing one opens on the night of the eighteenth into the nineteenth of Mudmonth and returns on the **twenty-seventh**; path time inside it is six days. The fever takes **three days — the twenty-seventh, twenty-eighth and twenty-ninth — and is over on the thirtieth**, which is the day Chapter 10 opens. The thirtieth is not a fourth day of illness and must not be called one; write it as the day the fever breaks. Three days is the figure in the crossing budget above and in `outline/volume-01.md`; `outline/ending.md` states no fever length and is not a source for it. The cost does not grow until Stage 6.
26. **The batch's day map is the only calendar the first ten chapters use.** Ch 1 the sixth · Ch 2 the eleventh · Ch 3 the twelfth · Ch 4 the night of the eighteenth into the nineteenth · Ch 5–7 path time with no main-world clock · Ch 8 the twenty-seventh · Ch 9 the twenty-seventh to the thirtieth · Ch 10 the thirtieth. The thirteenth to the eighteenth is a stated cut: no visitor, no document, no arrest, no further bell sound. **The bell stays on its cradle across that cut because of the twenty-fourth hold in item 15, not because a date was missed.** Volume 01's own time budget in `outline/volume-01.md` now lists the same days.
27. **Two guilds, two names, no crossing between them.** "The guild" in the main world is the **Merefen bridge guild**: a town's craft body of bridgewrights and road-wardens holding the Old Toll crossing in municipal trust, keeping the scrap roll the bell's six-mark valuation comes from. The path's institution is always the **Bellweather Bridge Guild**. They never appear in each other's world in Volume 01, and a chapter must not let "the guild" be ambiguous about which one it means.
28. **The promise Aren makes to Mara is undated.** She asks for a promise that the bell will not be rung, and refuses to put a date on it, so Chapter 4 is a broken promise rather than a technicality. Do not restore the earlier wording, which limited the promise to "before the renewal." The batch owns **two omissions, holding three facts**: to Mara, the promise with no date on it and the night he has already chosen; to Tovan, the reason he refused six marks and signed a bond. Both omissions appear in the written account he files in Chapter 10, which is why the second is what makes the first credible.
29. **The Volume 01 drift ceiling.** The first crossing's warning is wrong by six days and the third by three weeks. `outline/series.md` now allows three days to three weeks of uncertainty at return for Volume 01, and three weeks is the ceiling at this scale. Do not drift further in this volume, and do not drift by months.
30. **Chapter files, batch summaries, and review records have fixed homes.** Chapters go to `chapters/volume-01/chapter-0001.md`, four digits, matching the card labels `### Chapter 0001` in `outline/batches/volume-01-batch-0001.md` (`PHASE_SYSTEM.md` is the authority and is controller-owned; this entry records the convention where the writer will actually see it). A completed batch's summary is appended to `state/batch-summaries.md`, and the one-line "Last batch summary" field in `state/current.md` points at the most recent entry. Reviewer findings are persisted under `reviews/`, not left in `logs/`, which is gitignored.
31. **Dispatch guards, standing version of item 11.** Do not create a second prompt for a batch that already has one; if a phase's work tempts a new prompt directory, it is a new batch and not a new copy of this one. And **`state/phase-ledger.json` is controller-owned, is never written by the writer, is stale by design, and is not what dispatch reads** — the prompts themselves are the dispatch list, and `AGENTS.md` says to update the ledger while `.opencode/agent/novel-writer.md` forbids it. The runner's own restore list does not include the ledger, so nothing will undo a careless edit; the only protection is the agent rule and the fact that no one reads the field. Leave it alone: a writer who "fixes" it is the one most likely to break the one-prompt-per-batch protection.


## The window, at the Volume 13 close

**The window moved forward for Volumes 01 to 12 at the Volume 13 close, and it is the first move in this project that TRIMS the live file as well as copying it.** Every one of the five earlier moves, at the Volume 07, 08, 09, 11 and 12 closes, took an exact copy into `state/archive/` and then left the live file whole, and the live file grew back from the copy point every time. **The consequence of that convention, measured, is this: at the moment this cut was taken the five ledgers stood at 3.06 MB together, `state/continuity.md` at 984 KB holding every volume from Volume 09 forward, `state/open-threads.md` at 764 KB, `state/chapter-summaries.md` at 560 KB, `state/batch-summaries.md` at 468 KB, and `state/current.md` at 246 KB holding ten overlapping "What is true at the close of Chapter N" blocks for Chapter 560 through Chapter 650. That is roughly 780,000 tokens of state for a project whose live instruction is about 25,000 words, and Volume 14 cannot load it. The review of Volume 13 Batch 0005 recorded the arithmetic and called it a blocker, and it is one, and the fix is this cut.**

**Nothing is lost and nothing is renumbered.** `state/archive/continuity-volumes-01-12.md` is a byte-for-byte copy of this file as it stood immediately before this cut, so the entire pre-cut ledger, guides and all, is recoverable by concatenating the archive back onto the live file at the cut point, and item 412 and thread 412 and chapter 412 and batch 412 all mean exactly what they meant. **The cut moved whole sections only, at section boundaries, and it did not touch a single item number, thread number, chapter number or batch number.** What left the live file is: **the Volume 09, 10, 11 and 12 batch sections, every review-repair section, and both of those volumes' closes, which is `## The window, at the Volume 08 close` and every section from *Volume 09, Batch 0001* to *Volume 12 close* inclusive.**

**What stayed live, and rule 4 of `state/archive/README.md` is why.** `## Hard canon`, `## Ending constraints` and `## Knowledge control` are global and are in this file now whatever their byte offset, and so are the two canon-clarification sections and `## Initial story threads`, `## Answers to the questions the outline phase had to settle`, `## Final-volume questions` and `## Planning status` in the thread ledger. They are in the archive as well, because the archive is a copy, and a global section in both places is harmless and a global section in neither is not.

**The rule this cut changes, stated so that nobody inherits it by accident.** The old convention was *copy, and leave the live file whole*, and its stated justification was that a copy is safer than a deletion. **That is true and it is also what made the files unusable, and both halves cannot stand.** The new convention is *copy first, verify the copy, then trim the live file*, and the verification is one command: the archive must be byte-identical to the pre-cut live file, which is checked here against git rather than by reading. **The window still does not move in the middle of a volume.** It moved for Volumes 01 to 07 at the Volume 07 close, 01 to 08 at the Volume 08 close, 01 to 09 at the Volume 09 close, 01 to 10 at the Volume 11 close, 01 to 11 at the Volume 12 close, and 01 to 12 at this one, and it will not move again until Volume 14 is closed.

**What a Volume 14 writer reads, in this order, and does not read the rest of:** this guide, `state/current.md`, `outline/volumes/volume-13-close.md` in whole, and the threads this close hands on. Everything older is reachable by grep and is not to be read. **The highest item in this file is the newest, and `state/current.md` carries the figure.**

## Volume 13, Batch 0001 — items 1440 to 1451, and the highest number in this file is now 1451

**This section is appended under the three rules at the top of this file: items are never renumbered, nothing is deleted, and a volume is not archived while it is being written.** Items 1 to 1439 stand and are not renumbered. **This was a writing pass, not a repair pass: `chapters/volume-13/` was empty when it began, `outline/volume-13.md` was written in this pass, and Chapters 601 to 610 were drafted from it, then measured, then repaired where the measurements found defects.** Every figure below was counted on the ten chapter files after the last prose edit in this pass, and the basis is named in 1449.

1440. **The volume exists and its plan is `outline/volume-13.md`, and the volume is *The Law of Unopened Doors*, Chapters 601 to 650, day 1831 to day 2040.** Two hundred and nine days elapsed and two hundred and ten inclusive, seven months on the carried thirty-day rule, matching the Volume 13 row of the ledger in `outline/series.md`. **The year does turn inside this volume: day 1956 is the first of Thawmonth YR 321 and it is the last day of Block 0003, so a writer who carries YR 320 through the whole volume is wrong on 85 of its two hundred and ten days, because 125 of them are YR 320 and 85 are YR 321. The *twenty-eight* this item originally carried was wrong by fifty-seven and is corrected here and at `state/current.md` and in `outline/volume-13.md` §17; nothing in the prose was ever dated from it, because Batch 0001's and Batch 0002's datelines were derived off the anchor and not read out of it.** Five blocks of forty-two days: 1831 to 1872, 1873 to 1914, 1915 to 1956, 1957 to 1998, 1999 to 2040.

1441. **The forged sheet, and it is the volume's antagonist for its first half, and nobody wrote it on the page and nobody traced it.** It arrived in a band of about a hundred and four on the twenty-sixth of Embermonth YR 320 in `chapter-0601.md`. **Four hands of writing, no seal, nothing on the back, and a name at the top of the first page in the place where a name goes on a thing that is going to be acted on, and the name is Aren Kest.** It directs that the keeper of a certain road be stood down for nine days and that the road be shut for the same nine days, the matter being under question. The road is named by its two ends and nothing else: **the ferry stone at the low end, the ninth milestone at the top, and a ford between them that is dry in most summers.** It says neither by whom nor about what, and that is the ordinary way of writing.

1442. **The three findings of Block 0001, and they are the whole of the block.**

- **A document with a name at the top and no sender is obeyed, and the reason it is obeyed is true.** The man of about fifty-eight with a badge says it out loud in `chapter-0602.md` and refuses to call it false: *a thing with a name at the top of it is not false because the man in the room thinks it is false; it is false when somebody can show it to be false, and this room has no way of showing anything false.* And then he says the second true thing, which is worse: **that a direction obeyed because of a name and not because of an office can be written by anybody in nine counties with a pen, that in thirty-one years of the work it has never been done, and that he wants it on a bare leaf with his own name at the top of it.** The copyist refuses to put his name at the top, on the ground that she is not going to be the second person in nine counties to prove her own point, and seven words go on a bare leaf instead: *the man with the badge said it.*
- **The room's own virtue is the block's second engine, and the copyist says so before anybody finds it.** Three series — about nine hundred sheets a month out into nine counties, about a hundred and four in one band on a fixed day, about four hundred and nine parish rolls a month in off the roads — and **not one of the three is made out of another, and that is not tidiness, it is a wall put behind a road so that one bad return cannot be set beside one good return and made to look like a pattern.** Two sheets about the same four miles of the same channel, ten days apart in the writing and three weeks apart in the books, land in two different series, and one of them is a lie, and nobody in that room is permitted to put them side by side. `chapter-0603.md`.
- **A road with no number is not a light thing. It is a thing with no weight in it, and a gap is not carried by anybody, and a gap goes first, and nobody has to be cruel to close it.** That is said out loud by a man of about forty-seven who brought a page of seven lines in `chapter-0607.md`, and it is the truest sentence in the block, and the man of about thirty-four with a sheet wrote it down without being asked.

1443. **The seven, named once, in plain terms, in `chapter-0605.md`, and no figure for any of them and no distance.** **The ferry city; the county where a list of names was burned; the waterworks country; the coast of returning roads; the fruit valley; the glass country on the high flats; and the small one.** Two more are not on the list and are referred to only as not being on it: **the city under the light, which refuses, and the valley nobody can reach.** The block does not convert that list into a register, a roll, a sheet or a book, and the man with the badge asked for a list and the copyist asked who would be written at the top of it and **nobody wrote one.** The figure seven went on a bare leaf with no head over it and into the drawer about eleven inches deep.

1444. **The seven is also the limit, and he says the limit out loud and names nothing, and the naming is the point.** *Seven. And there is no eighth, and I have not tried for one, and I am not going to find out this month whether there is.* And the reason he gives is: **he has run out of a thing he cannot name, and the thing does not ask his permission and it does not tell him when it is finished, and he said it in a room rather than in a man's head, and cannot put it in the record.** **The mechanism is not named in any of the ten chapters, no chapter says what a roadmark is, no crossing is conducted or scheduled in this block, and the word *stage* and the word *ledger* and the word *qualification* and the word *panel* are at zero across all ten files.**

1445. **He answered for two of the seven and said out loud that he answered for the two he had been in.** The ferry city and the fruit valley, both of which have a person in them who can refuse him. **Three have a person he can write to and a person he cannot; one is a country that has never had a list of itself in anything; the fifth is the small one, and he cannot say one fact about the small one in that room without somebody writing it down and it being true for a week.** The man of about fifty-eight with a badge said the true thing about that: **he has not chosen, he has been the last one there, and a man who has been last somewhere cannot be argued with at all.**

1446. **The sheet was obeyed, and what it cost is the whole moral weight of the block, and the cost is nothing anybody can point at.** The copy was cut to about nine lines and printed in a district hand and posted on a Friday and read out at the district end of the ferry stone by a gatekeeper with about eleven years at that gate. **He did not check the name, and the page says he did not, and says why: checking the name means asking who the man is, the man is not in the district, nobody in the district can say, and a gatekeeper who stops a road to ask a question he has no way of answering is a gatekeeper who has stopped the road.** The woman of about fifty who keeps that road for nineteen years was told at the gate and was not asked anything, was not shown the page, and went home for nine days and came back on the ninth day. **Nothing rotted. Nobody was late. Nobody was hurt. No wages went anywhere, because she is paid in kind and the kind is a season's slack.** `chapter-0608.md`.

1447. **The nine days ran from 1861 to 1870 on the calendar of the block, and the whole of what it cost is a sentence the woman of about fifty came four days of carting to say, and it is not the nine days.** *For nine days, nobody in about nine houses at the top end and about nine at the bottom knew whether I was stood down or gone home for a bad week. That is the whole of it.* And the reason there was no arrangement: **the standing down came off a gate, the gate said it to her, the gate did not write to her, and the page has her name nowhere on it and has a man's name four days off on it who has not been in the county in four years.** `chapter-0609.md`.

1448. **The block's gift, and it is the question the Volume 12 close said nobody had asked.** A woman of about forty who runs a lime kiln asked a man of thirty-two with a limp **do you want it** — twice, the second time in a working voice, with a barrow in her hands and a boy of about eleven four paces off with a sledgehammer up — and he did not answer. **The reason is on the page and it is not a reason about a certificate: he had about nine seconds of a slope in which the answer he had was a yes, and a yes said on that slope at the sixth hour would have made him the seventh thing on the page she had just described, and a man who has been the only person who can see the load-bearing problem in a place three times running has been right three times and has never once been asked whether he wanted it.** `chapter-0610.md`. **The volume does not open the answer until Block 0003, where the answer he gives is a closure.**

1449. **The block recounted after the last prose edit, every basis named.** Plain whitespace split, title line included, each file on its own and the ten added: **20,333 words, per chapter 1,981 / 1,742 / 1,753 / 2,154 / 2,144 / 2,106 / 2,268 / 1,922 / 2,228 / 2,035**, and scene bodies, located structurally as everything from the paragraph above the item list to the end of the file, **1,603 / 1,362 / 1,365 / 1,739 / 1,744 / 1,703 / 1,871 / 1,515 / 1,816 / 1,609**, minimum 1,362 at `chapter-0602.md` and maximum 1,871 at `chapter-0607.md`, against Volume 12's range of 1,161 to 2,095. **Volumes 01 to 12 are 1,681,701 in six hundred chapters and do not move, and the manuscript is 1,702,034 in six hundred and ten chapters, and 1,681,701 + 20,333 reproduces it.**

1450. **The ten money columns, footed on four renderings, and the prior set beside the number as `state/open-threads.md` 427 and 428 require.** **214, 232, 198, 245, 249, 182, 217, 260, 225 and 213 pence**, one in every chapter, eight to twelve items a column (8, 11, 8, 12, 10, 9, 11, 8, 12, 10), **every one footing on item sum, restated price list, printed total and mark conversion at forty-eight pence a mark.** Ten distinct totals, no duplicated price inside a column, **no price above forty-one, no price of thirty-six, and no barred numeral in any total, over-figure or printed price.** Collision check: **the prior set was rebuilt in this pass as the union of two extractors over all six hundred chapters before this block and is 139 distinct; the ten totals collide with none of it, and the set size is printed beside the claim because a collision report without its set is not a measurement.** Calendar re-derived from day 930 with the day-of-month half and the weekday half derived separately: **1831, 1837, 1841, 1846, 1851, 1854, 1858, 1861, 1870 and 1872, 0 mismatches on either half, five market days at 1837, 1846, 1851, 1858 and 1872, and two Sundays at 1854 and 1861 on which the room over the weigh-house is shut.**

1451. **The devices, and the one observation this pass made about a chapter it is forbidden to repair.** `about` **519 in 20,333, which is 25.53 per 1,000**, on a case-insensitive whole-word numerator over a whitespace denominator, against Volume 12's 24.88. `the whole of` **22**, `about four people` **7**. The seven filler words at **23**: `held` 2, `steady` 0, `plain` 2, `counted` 0, `breathed` 0, `wrote` 19, `nodded` 0. Punctuation **16 question marks, 0 exclamation marks, 0 semicolons, 0 em dashes, 0 en dashes**, and a question asked aloud takes one, which is the correction at Volume 10's item 154. **Curly quotation marks 228 and 228, straight double quotes 0, curly apostrophes 0 and straight apostrophes 42**, and the form `“**text**”` is in every speech. Barred families `failed` `seam` `roof` `coping` `Gatehouse` `custodian` `melody` `Culling` `eastern` `hemp` `charter` `brother` `stage` `ledger` `qualification` `panel` `system` `protagonist` `council` `hundredweight` `mechanic` `attribute` `status` `level` at **0**, the retired reporting frame at **0**, and `continuation` at **0**. **Exact duplicate paragraphs at twelve words or more, the ten new against each other and against the six hundred before: 0 and 0, which is the correct result for both.** Thank-family **28** and **every one is a negation or is about four houses owing a lime bill.** `Aren Kest` **45 across all ten and in every chapter.** Name budget **2 of 5, three slots unspent**: `Nell Craddock` 8, `Garvin Slade` 12, and a capitalised two-word sweep over the ten files returns those two and nothing else. **The observation, made and not acted on: `chapter-0600.md`'s cost coda prints *eight figures of time* and its list is nine. ## Volume 13, Batch 0002 — items 1452 to 1463, and the highest number in this file is now 1463

**This section is appended under the three rules at the top of this file: items are never renumbered, nothing is deleted, and a volume is not archived while it is being written.** Items 1 to 1451 stand and are not renumbered. **This was a writing pass and not a repair pass: Chapters 611 to 620 were drafted, then measured, then four prose defects were found and repaired in place, and no chapter was restarted and no plot beat was moved.** Every figure below was counted on the ten chapter files after the last prose edit in this pass, and the basis is named in 1462.

1452. **The block exists and the ten days are 1896, 1900, 1902, 1904, 1907, 1909, 1910, 1911, 1913 and 1914, derived from the single anchor and 0 mismatches on either half.** The anchor is **day 930, the twenty-fifth of Mudmonth YR 318 and a Sunday**, on the carried thirty-day month, and the day-of-month half and the weekday half were derived from the day number separately and never from one another. **Block 0002 is days 1873 to 1914 inclusive, forty-two days, that is the eighth of Frostmonth YR 320 to the nineteenth of Hearthmonth YR 320, and every date in it is YR 320, because the year turns at day 1956 and that is the last day of Block 0003.** Five of the ten are market days, at 1900, 1902, 1907, 1909 and 1914, and two are Sundays, at 1896 and 1910, and the room over the weigh-house is shut on both, and 1896 is the first of a month and is the first chapter of the block. **No day of the block falls between two chapters.** **And this pass found that the printed table at `outline/volume-13.md` §17 is wrong in five rows and is internally inconsistent after day 1872; it puts 1896 on a Monday where the chain gives a Sunday, and the day-of-month and month-start rows in that table were not used. The block's own datelines were derived and then checked against the anchor, and the outline was not edited by this phase.**

1453. **The page, which is the block's object, in its finished form.** A larger sheet folded in two, printed twice, with **six ruled spaces against six lines and a seventh line bare and not struck out**, and it went round nine counties and came back. **A mark in a space is not a vote for the arithmetic. It is a statement by a person who can be named and found and asked what their figure is, and that is the only reason any mark on that page is worth anything.** By the close of the block the six spaces stand at **three with nothing in them, of which about four are refusals and one is a line the page has not reached, and the page cannot tell them apart, and three with a mark in them, one of which carries nineteen columns of a woman's own count ruled under the line in a different hand with a nine taken out of the last one in a third hand.** **A page has no way of holding a refusal, so a refusal comes back as a blank, and a blank is indistinguishable from a page nobody has reached, and by the second time round that indistinguishability is permanent.** Nobody named who wrote it, nobody traced it, and no chapter said it was written by a person with an office.

1454. **The four rules this block is built on, in the order they are used, and none of them is a summary of anything.** **A blank is not a no, and a road everybody has refused has been shut by a printing press.** **A figure about a road is the only thing anybody can be argued with, and the room cannot supply one and refuses correctly and the refusal helps nobody.** **A rule with no head on it is worth exactly nothing, however right it is.** **And a person who has offered one thing once, for a reason, about her own road, has not started anything anybody else may continue.**

1455. **A man of about thirty-one from the road office, and what the room's own practice is when it is read correctly by somebody outside it.** He came with a letter and no coat and asked what the room's practice is, and got the answer that three series are kept and none is made out of another, and then said the reading, and it is correct: **a rule that keeps three series apart is a rule that says no two returns may be read together, and a page that wants six marks is a thing that cannot be read any other way, because there is nothing else in nine counties to read it against, and so the rule is a wall in front of the only door there is.** **The copyist of about twenty-six was not defended and did not want to be. She said it was a correct reading, that she had had four years to make it and had not, that she made the rule for a different reason and the reason still holds, that a pattern is a thing a district can be shown, and that she is not going to change anything.** **And she said the cost out loud in a room: four years of correctly kept series is the reason two sheets about the same four miles of channel could sit in two books for three weeks and one of them is a lie, she found that out in the first week of a month, she did not tell anybody, she laid them side by side once on a counter and put them back, and there is no second practice in that room to put in the place of the one she has just said is a blind spot, and she has been looking for a month.** Nobody in that room disagreed with her and nobody defended her and about four of the nine understood and about five did not.

1456. **Aren Kest has read his own name at the top of the forged page, and has done one thing about it, and that was to do nothing.** The man of about thirty-four with a sheet showed him a copy of the sheet in his own writing, one hand, made at the counter on a Thursday and carried to the district on the Friday and kept in a satchel for about a month, and at the top of the first page in the place where a name goes on a thing that is going to be acted on is his own name in a hand that is not his. **He read it twice and he did not say the writing was his and he did not say it was not his.** The page gives three reasons and the third is the one that costs: **a man who says a page is not his is making a claim, a claim has to be believed, and being believed is a thing a person is given and not a thing a person does; and about four hundred people in that town hold one true thing about him and about four hundred hold another, the two sets do not overlap, and he said no when he was asked whether he would put the two of them straight, and a man who has declined to correct a record cannot afterwards be surprised that the record is used.** The one thing he could have done was say it out loud in that market, and he told the man with the sheet that he was not going to and why, and the why is on the page and it is not modesty. **The man of about thirty-four with a sheet then wrote on the back of the copy, unasked, that the man named was shown the copy on the fourteenth of a month at about the second hour and did not say the writing was his, and that record is in a satchel and is in no book, and nobody thanked him for it.**

1457. **The woman of about fifty, her count, and what she said at a gate on the eighteenth of a month.** She keeps the road from the ferry stone at the low end to the ninth milestone at the top, nineteen years, about nine households at each end and a ford dry in most summers. **Her count is nineteen columns, one for each year, of the days that road was passable out of the days in that year, and the last column is unfinished because the year is unfinished, and a nine has been taken out of it in a hand that is not hers.** She read that count out loud at the gate at the low end, in the open, in front of about nine people and a carter and a man from the district, and put a mark in the seventh space of the page in the same breath, and gave the reason in a working voice: **an empty space is a road nobody is carrying, and she would rather be argued with than be left alone on a page.** She said three further things and none of them may be softened: **a count shuts nothing and is only the thing anybody can be argued with; she has offered hers once, at a gate, about her own road, on a day when a page came round, and one time is one time and nobody may cite it; and she still does not know which way her count runs and would like somebody in that county to tell her, and nobody has.** **The man of about fifty-eight with a badge stood at that gate for half an hour and said nothing at all, and the page gives the reason and it is not modesty: she was about to be argued with in the open about a figure, and he was not going to be the man who interrupted it to talk about a page with a man's name at the top of it four days off.**

1458. **The second cost of the block is a hand in an apron, and it is the copyist's, and it is new.** On a Tuesday in the first week of a month, at the counter under the weigh-house, the woman of about fifty put a slate down and did not open it and said she had taken nine days out of the last column in a hand that was not hers, and **the copyist of about twenty-six wrote nine into that last column with her own pen about ten minutes later, did not tell her, and did not tell anybody for about six weeks.** She said on the nineteenth of a month, in a room with about nine people in it and unasked, that she did not know which way the woman's count runs and the woman did not know either, and that there was nobody in that room who knew, and that she is a person who keeps figures in columns and puts a figure she does not understand at the end, and that that is a habit and not a reason. **She said she was telling the woman because the count had been read out loud at a gate by a man with a page and a person who has put a figure into another person's count ought to say so before anybody uses the figure, and nobody asked her and she was not going to let it be found out by somebody else.** Nobody thanked her and nobody told her she was wrong, and **the answer the room gave is that it stands where it is, in the wrong place, in a hand that is not hers, until somebody who knows which way the count runs says otherwise, and that this is the correct answer and a bad answer and the only one.**

1459. **The separation, planted twice and repaired in neither, and the shape of the whole volume.** The keeper of the room did not come down to the lane on two market mornings running. **The copyist of about twenty-six said so out loud in front of about nine people on both mornings and nobody in that room agreed with her on either morning, and she said once that she is not going to say it a third time.** What she put on the page is the finding: **nothing in that room depends on the woman who keeps it being in it; three series would go on being kept correctly for a month with her out of the lane; the copyist has wanted a room like that since she was about nineteen; and she is saying it in front of the woman.** **The two of them were in one room with a third person in it in none of the ten chapters.** He is in the room in 0612 to the fifth hour and she comes up at the seventh hour; he is in the lane in 0612, 0616 and 0620 and she goes past him; he is four days off the made road in 0613, 0615 and 0619; in 0611 they are both at a counter on a Sunday, which is a different room under a different door and is the one place in the block where they are in a space together with about nine other people, and **nothing passes between them in it.** **The four and a half years were not referred to as four and a half years about the two of them in any of the ten chapters, nothing between them was described in a document, and no finding is under the eighth question and there is not going to be one from him.** The woman of about thirty-four who keeps the key said once more, in 0615, that she had not been asked which roads were open and was not asking anything, and nobody told her she was wrong.

1460. **The eighth entry, and the one standing change this block makes to a room that had not moved in four and a half years.** **The register stands at eight entries, all of them questions, and none of them a finding. The eighth is the question a woman of about fifty asked in the first week of a month, which is whether a keeper can be told before a page about her road is read at a gate, and which the man of about fifty-eight with a badge answered in a room with the door open by saying that there is no way to agree it.** **The day-book stands at nine and the eighth question went into the register and not the day-book and the copyist would not say why and was not asked twice.** The press stands open with no date and nothing was printed. **The fourth head of the Crown's form is ruled and empty and about eight months empty and nobody has filled it and nobody may rule a fifth.** The half leaf is still face down. **The drawer about eleven inches deep holds about four hundred returns and now also a leaf with a question on it from the first of a month, a leaf with a standing of four and a half years on it, and a leaf describing a page with six ruled spaces and no head.**

1461. **SUPERSEDED IN PART BY 1471 AND 1475. The device counts in this item were true before the repair pass and the word counts are now 24,843, the curly quotes 290 and 290, the thank family 33, and every device listed here is unchanged by the repair. This item is left standing under its own number and is not to be read as a current figure. What did not move, and the devices.** The half leaf is face down and unturned and unscheduled and no interval is printed for it. The floor of the room was washed in Greenmonth YR 320 by somebody nobody knows and nobody was thanked and no interval is printed. The hearing stands with no bench in it. The shut road four hundred miles off is not reopened and nobody was sent and **the word *failed* is used of nothing in this volume.** The valley of about four hundred people is unreachable and unscheduled. About eleven hundred people are on no roll and no sheet and no list. The city on a shelf is not on a page and its three protected numerals are at zero. The coast with the twenty-two notches is not visited and the twenty-second is not cut. The bar of wood at the Low Gate is not lifted and the four questions on its board are not asked. **The warm left palm is not mentioned once in this block, the notebook's separate leaf with a seventh line that says *a person* is not looked at, the mechanism is not named, and no chapter says what a roadmark is.** The small road's count stayed in a boy of about eleven and **no chapter counted its people, printed a figure for it, or converted its count into main-world days.** The three series were not merged and the copyist went on keeping them correctly. The two readings of a man who published his own mistake are not chosen between in any chapter. **Aren Kest said the block's argument out loud in 0617, to about four people at a counter, in a working voice, and it did not make a thing true, and the chapter gives the reason and it is not modesty: a thing said in a lane by a man who can see the load-bearing problem in a place is a direction, and a direction is a page with a name at the top of it, and he has watched one shut a road for nine days.** Barred families, the four barred numerals, `continuation` and the retired reporting frame at **0** across the ten files. Punctuation **1 question mark, 0 exclamation marks, 0 semicolons, 0 em dashes, 0 en dashes**. **Curly quotation marks 278 and 278, straight double quotes 0, curly apostrophes 0 and straight apostrophes 44**, and the form `“**text**”` is in every speech. `about` **656 in 23,823, which is 27.54 per 1,000**, against 25.53 for Batch 0001. `the whole of` **25**, `about four people` **10**. Thank-family **31 and every one is a negation or is a statement that a person had not said a thing in order to be thanked.**

1462. **SUPERSEDED IN PART BY 1471, 1472 AND 1475. The figures in this item were true of the files as they stood before the repair pass and the block is now 24,843 and the manuscript 1,726,877, the collision set is 203 and not 167, and the long run is forty-five under the normaliser named at 1472. This item is left standing under its own number and is not to be read as a current figure. The block recounted after the last prose edit, every basis named.** Plain whitespace split, title line included, each file counted on its own and the ten added: **23,823 words in ten chapters, per chapter 2,757 / 2,403 / 2,324 / 2,240 / 2,189 / 2,381 / 2,206 / 2,120 / 2,402 / 2,801, and scene bodies 2,338 / 1,993 / 1,912 / 1,818 / 1,780 / 1,954 / 1,798 / 1,698 / 1,964 / 2,347, minimum 1,698 at `chapter-0618.md` and maximum 2,347 at `chapter-0620.md`.** Volumes 01 to 11 recount to **1,588,852** in five hundred and fifty chapters and do not move, Volume 12 to **92,849** in fifty, Batch 0001 to **20,333** in ten, **and the manuscript is 1,725,857 in six hundred and twenty chapters, and 1,588,852 + 92,849 + 20,333 + 23,823 reproduces it, and a concatenated `cat` of all six hundred and twenty files returns the same 1,725,857 and no chapter file is missing its trailing newline, so the per-file and concatenated bases agree.** Ten money columns at **202, 215, 229, 234, 243, 251, 262, 271, 283 and 294 pence**, one in every chapter, nine to eleven items a column (10, 10, 9, 11, 9, 11, 9, 10, 11, 10), **every one footing on item sum, restated price list, printed total and mark conversion at forty-eight pence a mark.** Ten distinct totals, no duplicated price inside a column, no price above forty-one, **no barred numeral in any total, over-figure or printed price.** **Collision: the prior set was rebuilt as the union of three independent extractors over the six hundred and ten chapters before this block and is 167 distinct; the ten totals collide with none of it, and the set size and the extractor count are printed beside the claim.** Calendar **0 / 0 / 0** off day 930 with the day-of-month half and the weekday half derived separately, across 1896, 1900, 1902, 1904, 1907, 1909, 1910, 1911, 1913 and 1914, five market days and two Sundays on which the room is shut. **Exact duplicate paragraphs at twelve words or more, the ten new against the six hundred and ten before and against themselves: 0 and 0. The longest run of words any chapter of the block shares with the six hundred and ten before it is forty-two and not forty, and the first three are 42, 41 and 40 and every one of the three is in 0611 or 0616 and is the cost coda's own frame or its roster; below them sit two runs in the thirties that are the block's own deliberate scene recurrences, 36 words at 0616 against `chapter-0602.md` and 34 at 0614 against `chapter-0604.md`, and both are marked on the page as recurrences and neither is a lift.** `Aren Kest` **43 in ten of ten, every one of them a chapter he is in or is named in as absent from the room, and the two chapters at 1 are the two in which he is four days down the made road.** Name budget **2 of 5, three slots unspent, and this block spent none.**

1463. **The four prose defects this pass found and repaired, and the one instrument finding.** **One, `chapter-0612.md`'s cost coda printed a capitalised number word at the head of its time list — *and A Thursday* — which is the class the Volume 12 close found eleven of and which a capitalised two-word sweep cannot see; lower case, and the block's ten restated enumerations all open with a capital because they begin a sentence after the bold close, which is correct.** **Two, `chapter-0620.md` had a doubled emphasis marker, four asterisks, spliced into the middle of a paragraph, and a batch-close instrument would have read it as prose; removed.** **Three, the figures claimed in four cost codas did not foot against their own lists, and this item originally described that repair backwards for two of the four and the review corrected it on the page. The verified state of all twenty distance lists, all ten time lists and all ten person lists on the files as they stand is that every one of them foots, and the standing rule is that the list is right and the numeral is moved and never the other way round.** What was wrong and what was moved: **`chapter-0612.md`'s persons list holds ten and had claimed nine and now claims ten; `chapter-0618.md`'s distance list holds thirteen and now claims thirteen; `chapter-0616.md`'s persons list holds ten and had been moved *to* eleven, which did not foot, and now claims ten; and `chapter-0619.md`'s persons list holds ten and had been moved *to* eleven, which did not foot either, and now claims ten, with its trailing cross-reference moved from the ninth of them to the sixth because the man of about forty-seven with a page is the sixth item of a ten-item list and not the ninth.** **The two that a naive segment count cannot see are the two the review pass caught: 0616's list holds ten because *a man who keeps a coast road and counts in tides* is one figure of persons and not two, and 0619's holds ten because *a man from the district who carries paper and does not know what is on it* is one figure of persons and not two, and the second half of that phrase is a predicate and not a person at all. A person list must be counted by hand, clause by clause, because a splitter that breaks on every *and* will return eleven for both of these and will appear to confirm a numeral that does not foot.** **Four, and this is the only one that was a lift rather than a form, `chapter-0614.md` had closed with forty consecutive words lifted verbatim from `chapter-0604.md`'s closing paragraph, the same man at the same culvert at the same hour about five weeks apart in the story; the sentence was rewritten to say the same four facts in its own order and the run is gone from the top of the table, and a block that recites its own coda frame at forty words is left standing and a block that recites its own prose is not.** **The instrument finding: three independent extractors were needed where two had been used, because the footing-keyed extractor alone sees the 128 chapters in this manuscript whose coda words its conversion differently or spells its total in the *a hundred* form, and the union of all three over the six hundred and ten prior chapters is 167 against the 139 the Batch 0001 pass reported for two over six hundred. A successor must build the set with three and print all three counts.**

## Volume 13, Batch 0002, the review repair of `next-0007`, and the highest number in this file is now 1465

**This section is appended under the three rules at the top of this file: items are never renumbered, nothing is deleted, and a volume is not archived while it is being written.** Items 1 to 1463 stand under their own numbers. **This was a repair pass and not a writing pass: no chapter was restarted, no plot beat was moved, and no scene was rewritten. Four prose words and five records.** The review is `logs/next-0007.review.log` and its findings are six with one minor note.

1464. **The review's six findings, what was real, and what this pass did about each, in the order a successor needs them.** **One, the Batch 0003 prompt misdated the day before the year turn: it said *the twenty-ninth of Hearthmonth YR 320*, which is day 1924, and the day before the turn is day 1955, which is the thirtieth of Goatmonth YR 320 and a Wednesday. Real, fixed in the prompt, and it was thirty-one days out.** **Two, the same sentence claimed twenty-eight of the block's forty-two days were YR 321 and fourteen were YR 320. The chain gives one and forty-one: 1915 to 1955 are YR 320 and 1956 alone is YR 321. Real, fixed, and the *twenty-eight* was inherited from a volume-level figure that is itself wrong by fifty-seven, because the volume's 210 days are 125 in YR 320 and 85 in YR 321, not 28 and 182. Corrected in the prompt, at `state/current.md`, at 1440 above, and in `outline/volume-13.md` §17.** **Three, `chapter-0616.md` claimed eleven figures of persons over a list that holds ten, and `state/continuity.md` 1463 recorded the repair backwards, saying the list held eleven when the numeral was moved *to* eleven. Real, fixed in the page and in 1463, and the same defect was found and fixed in `chapter-0619.md`, which the reviewer did not flag because its own segment count returned eleven for a ten-item list; 1463 is corrected for both. Two words in the prose and one cross-reference.** **Four, the coda share of 11.4 per cent in `state/current.md` and in the prompt does not reproduce under any reading. Measured: Batch 0002's mean is 17.8 per cent over a range of 15.2 to 19.9, and Batch 0001's mean is 19.8 over 17.5 to 22.1. Real, corrected in both places, and the directional conclusion that the block is scene-heavier than its predecessor survives while the number does not.** **Five, and this is the finding that is not a number: the rule *coda share should not rise as the scene prose falls* was broken inside this block, and the cause is that Batch 0002's coda runs 408 to 454 words, so the share is a direct function of body length. 0611 at a body of 2,338 is 15.2 per cent and 0618 at a body of 1,698 is 19.9 per cent, and Batch 0001 is coupled the same way. No prose was changed: the honest repair is to record the coupling, and it is recorded in the Batch 0003 prompt as a choice the next writer must make deliberately rather than a rule it can satisfy by writing longer bodies.** **Six, *the longest run is forty words* understates by two. It is forty-two, `chapter-0616.md` against `chapter-0607.md`, the cost coda's own time-figure list, and 0616's list is identical in content and order to 0607's. Corrected at 1462 above and in the prompt, with the two deliberate scene recurrences below the frame runs named and classified so that a successor reading a run-length table does not mistake them for lifts.** **The minor note, that `state/phase-ledger.json` reads `phase-000-bootstrap` and *planned* while the repository is fourteen batches deep: it is controller-owned and was not touched, and the staleness is recorded in `state/current.md` instead so that no successor trusts it.**
