1546. **The four marks and what they are, in full.** Four pieces of lime the size of a fist at the height of a thumb, gone hard, in the joint of a stone, one at the ferry stone and one at each of three places up the run. **The highest is a foot and nine inches above the bed; the lowest is three inches below the crown of the sill at the third marker.** **They are where the water stops, not where the road is, and they are not a survey and not a map, and a Crown officer who knows the day in advance does not need a column, he needs four marks and a book.** **He did not print a figure for the column anywhere in this block and one boy counted it twice and gave two figures and gave neither.**

1547. **Tovan Kest's return, in full, because he has been off the page for four volumes.** **Twenty-nine, one of nine men in a yard four hundred yards up a road in another town, and the yard owns the crews.** He has not been in the same place as his brother since Mudmonth of YR 312. **He came down the lane on the eighth of Longlight with a barrow and a length of board and about four men, on a job for a road office that does not pay for work it has not ordered, on the strength of an order that came down the district with a name at the top of it, and he read it and did not comment on it.** **The false baulm was built wrong on purpose for a day the order named, he said so out loud, he drove the wedge himself so the load came off the soft trestle onto the hard one, and he put a stone on the end of it so that anybody could see where a mistake had been.** **He gave his brother no answer at all about a phrase he had begun and been stopped in the middle of.** **In `chapter-0660.md` the captain says out loud, in a street, that four bridge crews are working forward on the lane and that one of them is in this county and has taken a contract from his advance party, and that he is saying it because every person in the county has told him the truth.** **THE WORD IS PRINTED TWICE IN THIS BLOCK, both in Chapter 0654, both in a refusal of a bar, and the cap for the volume is four.**

1548. **The three facts about the market town that this block changed and a successor must not undo.** **One, the copies on the boards are a quarter of the instrument.** **Two, there is no figure for the column in nine counties and none is going to be given.** **Three, a road-keeper stood his own road down for two days in Longlight without shutting it, and it was obeyed, and it cost about four men two days and about nine households at the low end the week of the dry, and no clause in nine counties reached it.**

1549. **The measurements, recounted on the ten files as they stand, with the two bases named separately.** **The file total IS 31,848**, per chapter **3,752 / 2,933 / 2,582 / 3,240 / 3,683 / 2,984 / 3,336 / 2,830 / 2,845 / 3,660**, and a concatenated `cat` returns 31,848 and every file ends in a newline. **The scene bodies ARE 3,252 / 2,334 / 2,102 / 2,672 / 3,253 / 2,579 / 2,890 / 2,188 / 2,472 / 3,312**, minimum 2,102 at `chapter-0653.md`, and a body includes the title line and the dateline. `about` IS **18.66 to 27.87 per 1,000**, against Volume 13's four blocks at 20.03, 22.46, 25.63 and 27.60. **Zero em dashes, zero en dashes, zero semicolons, zero exclamation marks, zero single asterisks, zero straight double quotes and zero curly apostrophes**, curly doubles balanced in every file, and per-line bold parity even on every line of every file. **21 question marks and no chapter at zero.** Exact duplicate paragraphs at twelve words or more: **0 and 0.** **The longest shared run is 58 windows, which is 65 words, at `chapter-0655.md` against `chapter-0650.md`, and it is the deliberate word-for-word reprise of the right-to-refuse sentence and must not be repaired; the next three are 24, 14 and 11 windows.** `Aren Kest` is in all ten and the name budget for the block is 2 of 6: `Mara Vey`, `Salla Dunnock` and `Halvard Rees`, of which the first is a return recorded at 1538 and not a spend.

1550. **Where the manuscript is after this block.** **1,836,946 words in six hundred and sixty chapters**, on a plain whitespace split with the title line included, each file counted on its own and the six hundred and sixty added. **Volumes 01 to 13 are 1,805,098 in six hundred and fifty chapters and did not move.** Volume 14's first block is 31,848 in ten and the two figures add. **The name of the antagonist is printed once in six hundred and sixty chapters' worth of pages in this block and once before that, and it is the first time in about eleven years.** `Mara Vey` is at 9 and was at 0 across all fifty files of Volume 13. **The inherited age inconsistency is flagged and not acted on: Aren Kest is thirty-two on day 2310 of a calendar that puts him at forty-one, and this volume carries thirty-two forward because a batch phase that opens an age reconciliation in a volume of a march has changed a fact about the world for a reason unconnected to its plot.** The reconciliation belongs to a volume close that says it is doing one.

## Volume 14, Batch 0002 — items 1551 to 1560, and the highest number in this file is now 1560

1551. **The frame Batch 0002 was written to, derived and not copied, and the ten days.** The single anchor is **day 930, the twenty-fifth of Mudmonth YR 318, a Sunday**, on the carried thirty-day month, and the day-of-month half and the weekday half were each derived from the day number separately. **Block 0002 is days 2095 to 2148 inclusive, fifty-four days, that is the twentieth of Rainmonth YR 321 to the thirteenth of Fallowmonth YR 321, and every day of it is YR 321, because the year does not turn until day 2341.** Month starts inside the block: **Harvestmonth the first at day 2106 and Fallowmonth the first at day 2136.** **The ten chapter days are 2095, 2098, 2103, 2106, 2112, 2119, 2125, 2131, 2140 and 2148**, and their datelines are, in order: **Rainmonth the twentieth, a Wednesday, not a market morning, the room shut; Rainmonth the twenty-third, a Saturday, a market morning; Rainmonth the twenty-eighth, a Thursday, a market morning, in the second county; Harvestmonth the first, a Sunday, not a market morning, the first of a month, the hearing standing in the street; Harvestmonth the seventh, a Saturday, a market morning, on the lane; Harvestmonth the fourteenth, a Saturday, a market morning, at a gate in the second county; Harvestmonth the twentieth, a Friday, not a market morning, the room shut; Harvestmonth the twenty-sixth, a Thursday, a market morning, at the gate culvert; Fallowmonth the fifth, a Saturday, a market morning; and Fallowmonth the thirteenth, a Sunday, not a market morning, the room shut.** **Six of the ten are market mornings and the four that are not are a Wednesday, a Sunday, a Friday and a Sunday, and the room over the weigh-house was shut on all four and each of those four chapters says so on the page.** **2106 is the block's only first of a month** and it is a Sunday, and the copyist keeps the promise she made in Chapter 0662 by reading a road's letter out loud at the boards on it. The printed table at `outline/volume-13.md` §17 was not read for the derivation.

1552. **THE BLOCK'S ENGINE, and it is a sentence and not an event, and the whole block runs on it.** **The second clause is the one that is working, and it has moved a horse off a mile and a half in about nine seconds, and not one of the three clauses touches it.** The instrument requires all and every the persons, horses, carts, goods and labour of the district to give such aid to the officers of the Crown as those officers shall require, at such times and in such manner as the officers shall set out, and the badge-man says out loud in a market street on the twenty-third of Rainmonth that it is the only one of the three things anybody in that street has ever been moved by. **The mechanism the block discovers is politeness.** A man in a livery asks a woman for a horse and says that it was very good of her before she has got the word out, and a woman of about fifty-nine says afterwards that she would have said no and did not get as far as it, and the badge-man states the rule there is no clause for: a person being asked. **Nothing is shut anywhere in this block until Chapter 0670 and the taking is total from the first page.** **The finding is delivered once, in Chapter 0662, and it belongs to that chapter: Chapter 0661 was written with a second, clause-by-clause statement of it and the review-repair pass cut that, so that 0661 ends on the first hand report it was given to carry and 0662 owns the discovery. A successor may not put the three-clause walk back into 0661.**

1553. **The small road's letter, in full, because a road keeping a count is now on the page twice and the conversion rule holds.** A leaf folded in two, written on both sides in a good hand, about nine words to a line, **carried by a man of about thirty-one whom nobody in nine counties knows, hired at a posting house at the top of a lane four days off, given the letter at a gate and told only that it was wanted at the market town and to look for the weigh-house.** The horse is a bay gelding of about nine hands, off the mile and a half where the made road stops being made, taken on the Monday of the twenty-second of Rainmonth at about the ninth hour by four men in a livery, **and it belonged to nobody in particular, which the letter insists is not the same as belonging to the road, and that is the only part of it anybody will try to get wrong.** **A horse off that mile and a half is a horse off about nine households, and about nine households is the whole of us.** **The count is nine crossings open and four shut weeks in two years, kept on a stick about the length of a boy's arm by a boy of about eleven, with a copy in his own hand in case, and his mother has the other at the back of a dresser, and neither has ever been shown to anybody.** **The letter asks for nothing and ends: we are not writing to ask you for the horse. We are writing because in about four years there will be a man who says that no horse was taken off that lane, and there will be nine of us who say there was, and he will have a book and we will not. That is the whole of what we want. We want somebody in this county to write down that there was a horse.** **The count is not converted into days by anybody in the block, including the man of about thirty-four with a sheet in his coat, and the badge-man says out loud that he is glad and gives the reason in nine words.** The copyist reads it at the boards on the first of a month and nowhere else in the volume so far.

1554. **The refusal of the register, and it is a different refusal from Chapter 0652 and the standing is perfect.** In Chapter 0652 the copyist refused a question asked by a person from outside the nine counties on the ground of standing. **Here the letter is from a road inside the nine counties and one of the seven, and she says out loud in a market street that the standing is the best standing anything has ever had, and she refuses anyway, and she gives a new reason: that a book which roads write to is a head at the top of thing, that a woman of about twenty-nine said that in Mudmonth of last year, that she has had about four and a half years to find an argument against it and has not got one, and that she wants about nine people to notice that the first letter any of the seven has ever sent anybody in this county has been refused by a drawer.** She also says, and it is the only figure-shaped thing she says about herself, **that she has wanted a letter for about four years, has said so out loud about four times to about nine people, has had none, and has just found out that she does not want one, and would have found that out in about four days if one had come, and that is worth more than the letter.** The letter goes on a leaf with no head over it and into the drawer about eleven inches deep. **No finding is entered under any of the nine questions and no tenth question is ruled.**

1555. **`Maud Trenchard`, 56, of the district, and she is the one name this block spends.** A Crown clerk of about six years who **drafted the instrument that is on the boards in the market town and in nine other places**, and who has come down two days with a bag because a man in a livery wrote a nine-word line to a room in the district saying that this county has put up the sheet, has not complied, and does not appear to intend to. **She is named by herself, in a cold room, to one witness, on the twenty-eighth of Rainmonth, and she says out loud that she has said her own name in a room about nine hundred times and would like it said in this one, and Aren Kest tells her what a name does inside a week, and neither of them pretends it is a courtesy.** She has grey at the front of her hair and none at the back, a stiff right hand, nine pages of a fair copy in a bag, and a want, said out loud for the first time to a living person: **that nothing she drafts should ever be obeyed by anybody who has not chosen to obey it.** The sentence she is honest about is the ninth line of the second page: **stand taken from the hour of this sheet, which hour is the hour of the sealing of it**, which she was told was a matter of form, and which she now says is the difference between a thing a person agrees to and a thing a person is already inside of, and that about five weeks ago every person in nine counties was already inside it and nobody asked them and she put them there in about four days and has not been right since. **She cannot leave the fair copy anywhere: a county is not a place a paper is kept.** **She is not a villain and she is not safe, and she says so herself, and the reason she gives is that she would draft the next one and is not going to refuse and is not going to pretend she has.** She asks a man of about forty-four where a road is kept and writes down on the back of the ninth page that there is a room with a door on a lane in a town two days off, and she asks twice whether she may write it down. **She never says the name at the top of the sheet and calls it the Marshal's hand.**

1556. **The second hole, named in the open by a person who is not the protagonist, and neither hole called small.** The woman of about thirty-six, on the first of Harvestmonth, at the hearing in a Sunday market street: **the three clauses are about a man doing a thing to a road, and a paper does not do a thing to a road and does not want a witness and is not a burden, and there is nobody in nine counties whose business it is to say that a paper has no business here.** She adds the extension that is new to this block: **the paper has turned into a man, and a man at a gate can be answered, and the answer goes in a small book, and there is not one clause about a book and not one about a person who has been asked and has answered without anybody having offered them the chance to say no first.** She says twice in that street that it is not a small thing and that she will not have anybody stand in that street and say it is one. **The first hole, that the clauses cover roads that can be written to, is restated by her and not developed, and no road that cannot be written to is named, visited, sent to, or resolved, and the word is not used about anything but the rule.**

1557. **The cost of the block, in full, and it is a person, and the manner is the manuscript's.** On the seventh of Harvestmonth a man in a livery came down the mile and a half, asked the road's own people for a barrow, was given one in about nine seconds, did not pay, did not think it needed paying for, and said that it was very good of them and went up at four miles an hour. **A young man of about twenty-two, a carter's mate out of a yard at the low end of the market street, went with the barrow because it weighs forty pounds empty, and said he would be back before dark.** **The barrow came down the lane at about the ninth hour, which is dark by the end of Harvestmonth, with one handle wet and the high side dry and the lad not on it, and nobody on the road saw it come, because there is no road on that lane for a mile.** **The crew's own account, which is the only evidence there is, and which a successor may not contradict: the man with the crew says the lad came up the made road behind them at about the fourth hour of the afternoon and went on with them about a mile to a gate that is on no map any of them has, and that at about the seventh hour, when the crew turned off the coast road and went to quarters at the ferry stone, the lad was not with them, and that it was not entered in the book because there is nothing in the book for a boy on a road, and then he corrects himself out loud and says that the half he has been asked about four times in a fortnight is the half he has not got, and that the barrow came down the lane without him and he did not see that either.** He was not got back. Nobody was killed and nobody has been told anything and his name is on nothing. **The absence on the page: a road-keeper who is not at something, which is the man of about fifty-one not at the top of the cut on a Friday and a man of about thirty-eight filling in without knowing what he was filling in for; a carter who says nothing has come from the end of it, which is the carter of about fifty and his fourteen days of turning a thing over and not being able to make it into a person; and a badge-man who asks once and does not ask twice, and says out loud why, which is that a man who asks a second time does not like the answer he has got.** **The nine reasons are read out in the open on a Friday and the ninth of them is that every one of the eight is a reason and not one of them is a person.** This road is the mile and a half where the made road stops being made and it is **not** the road that went quiet in Thawmonth, that road is not named, visited, sent to, or resolved anywhere in this block, and the two are not described in any vocabulary a reader could confuse.

1558. **The third thing from the ferry city, in full, and why it is worse for the county than an army.** On the fifth of Fallowmonth a woman of about forty-four who keeps a crossing four hundred miles off, not the woman who wrote in Chapter 0657, came through the market town on her way back, has been on the road about nine days each way, is going back that afternoon, and says out loud in a market street of about four hundred people that her city received the sheet on the ordinary post with a covering letter about a drain, that nobody in its council has been asked anything and nobody has instructed anybody, that its council met for about nine minutes on the second day after and **resolved that if anybody in nine counties asks a city of ours which place or road a paper of the Crown is meant, this city will answer, and will name the place, and will not refuse to name it**, and that the reason given is that the city's crossing keeps its count in crossings open and weeks shut, that it is the same count, that its rule about roads being shut is a good rule and is not a small thing, and that the sheet says any person asked which place or road is meant shall answer and the city would rather say the name itself than have it taken out of it in a room. **It is not an army and it is not a refusal to be an army, and she says so: a thing said in a room about a county makes that county a thing being talked about, and nine people here would like it very much to be a thing being talked to somebody, and she has come nine days to say that it is not going to be.** **The badge-man's finding: nine counties cannot refuse a thing a city says about it, and no clause in nine counties is about a thing being said about a road by somebody four hundred miles off in a room nobody can get into.** **And it is the first thing anybody has brought this county that cannot be said no to, and it did not come with a man in a livery.** A question is asked aloud in that market on that morning and nobody answers it, and the words are these: **Then the question is whether to ask them.** Nobody has been able to establish who said it and four people have given four different trades for it. **The protagonist is four miles down the lane and is told at about the sixth hour by the man of about thirty-four, who says he did not send for him and would not have, and who then says the second thing that matters: that they have said we will not be asked again this year, that a woman who keeps a crossing four hundred miles off will be asked by somebody in about nine days, that it will not be the Crown because the Crown asked and got a no, and that the only people in nine counties who can get there are four.** **Aren Kest's answer is that he is five hours late for the market and on time for the crossing, and that there is no arrangement in nine counties that would have put him in the room where a county thinks about a thing.**

1559. **The climax's finding, and it is the hardest form the volume's argument takes, and it is on the page in a lane at a ford.** On the twelfth of Fallowmonth, which is a Saturday and a market morning and the day before the chapter, Halvard Rees's forward party asked at the boards which place or road is meant, in front of about four hundred people, and nobody answered, and a serjeant entered **asked at the boards, the twelfth of Fallowmonth, and no place given**, and the captain read it back. **The thirteenth is the Sunday the chapter is written on and it is not the day the question was asked, and a successor may not move the entry to the thirteenth.** On the thirteenth, a Sunday, a chain of about thirty feet went across the ford on two posts at about the seventh hour. **The captain comes down the bank on foot and says four things and none of them is a boast.** One: he has not shut the ford, he has taken it, and a thing that is shut has an end to it and everybody can stand at it, and a thing that is taken has no end to it and no moment. Two: the paper compels an answer, he asked, nobody answered, the line is going to be read out in a room with a table in it in about four years by a man who is not a knave. Three: **he has read the three clauses twice, has had nine men read them, cannot find anything in them that stops him, has looked for nine days, and says in a lane that he did not look and that he looked.** Four: he asks the woman of about fifty which place or road is meant. **And the woman of about thirty-six gives the finding out loud: none of the three is broken and the ford is shut, because the notice wants a man who proposes to shut a road and nobody proposed, the witness wants somebody named by the people who work the road and a man of about twenty-six could have named a man of about seventeen, and the burden wants a man who said a road may be shut and there is no such man, and so the burden falls on nobody.** **The rule is working exactly as nine people made it, because the rule is about a man who proposes, and a man carrying out a paper is not a man who proposes.** **And the decision is the woman of about fifty's, given before about nine people in the rain at about the eighth hour: nobody thought to ask her whether she wanted to give the name of her own four miles, she gives it, she says the cost of it out loud, that a road with a name can be argued with and a road without one can only be taken, and she says the figure she once refused to let be made into a rule, which is that in the worst week four in ten of the households at the two ends of her four miles cannot go round, and that four houses at the low end of that lane are under about a foot of water.** The captain writes it down at once and reads it back in the way he read a board back at a gate. **Nobody thanked anybody.** The chain stayed on its two posts.

1560. **THE MEASUREMENTS, recounted on the ten files as they stand, with the two bases named separately.** **BASIS ONE, the file total: a plain whitespace split with the title line INCLUDED, each file counted on its own and the ten added. The block IS 34,987**, per chapter **3,546 / 3,760 / 3,768 / 3,445 / 3,265 / 3,357 / 3,367 / 3,520 / 3,433 / 3,526**, and a concatenated `cat` of the ten returns 34,987 and every file ends in a newline, so the two bases agree. **BASIS TWO, the scene body: everything above the paragraph that opens the cost coda, which is the file total less the cost line less the money column paragraph, and which THEREFORE INCLUDES THE TITLE LINE AND THE DATELINE, so a body is not a scene body. The bodies ARE 3,077 / 3,373 / 2,987 / 3,122 / 2,916 / 3,055 / 3,032 / 3,134 / 3,095 / 3,047**, minimum 2,790 at `chapter-0666.md`, and a body includes the title line and the dateline. Codas are **469 / 387 / 781 / 323 / 349 / 302 / 335 / 362 / 338 / 479**. **`about` IS 18.05 to 27.09 per 1,000** over the ten, and the shortest chapter by this measure is 0663 at 18.05, which is at the floor of the house band and is the only one of the ten below twenty-four. **The money columns were re-cut across all ten by the review-repair pass because eight of the ten had ended on the same two items at the same two prices, and the ten price sets now share at most four values with each other and no two adjacent chapters share a tail.** **Zero em dashes, zero en dashes, zero semicolons, zero exclamation marks, zero single asterisks and therefore no italics, zero straight double quotes and zero curly apostrophes**, curly doubles balanced in every file, per-line bold parity even on every line, and 2 / 3 / 2 / 3 / 1 / 3 / 1 / 2 / 4 / 2 question marks, **twenty-three in all, with no chapter at zero.** `Aren Kest` is in all ten. **Exact duplicate paragraphs at twelve words or more are 0 against the 660 prior files and 1 inside the block, and the one is deliberate: the small road's letter is read out in Chapter 0662 and read out again in Chapter 0664 on the first of a month, because the copyist said in Chapter 0662 in a market street in front of about four hundred people that she would read it at the boards on the first of every month for as long as there was a horse in it. That is a promise kept and not a duplicate, and it is 71 windows, which is 78 words, of consecutive eight-word windows between those two chapters.** Against the 660 prior files the longest shared runs are **24, 22, 19, 16, 15** windows, at `chapter-0661.md`, `chapter-0662.md`, `chapter-0666.md`, `chapter-0664.md` and `chapter-0668.md`, all of them house recurrences, and the 24 is the market street and the man with the bar and the 22 is the second clause read out loud. **MONEY, with the extractor first and in the standing order. The scope is all 660 chapter files before this block, read whole and not sliced to the last two paragraphs, which is the change that makes the set reproducible. Extractor one, the printed total, clause-anchored on `is <spelled number> pence.` and taken at a parsed value of 60 or above, returns 188 instances and 171 distinct. Extractor two, the restated total inside `and <spelled number> pence is <spelled number> marks and <spelled number> over`, returns 172 instances and 163 distinct, and is NOT a strict subset of extractor one, because extractor two also reaches totals below sixty. The union is 177 distinct. The four barred numerals appear nowhere in it, so the free set is 177 out of 177. Column count: ten. Collision result: zero.** **180 of the 660 prior files carry a printed total at or above 60 under this extractor, and the figures recorded at 1549 for Block 0001 were built on a different scope and a different extractor and are not reproduced by this one, which is why this item prints its own.** The ten totals are **196, 210, 235, 250, 264, 276, 287, 300, 322 and 343**, ten distinct, ascending, each footing four ways, ten items a column, ten distinct prices a column from 1 to 41, **and the price sets after the repair-repair pass are 2/6/15/16/17/18/21/25/37/39, 1/4/11/12/13/27/32/34/36/40, 7/9/13/19/23/29/30/31/35/39, 6/8/22/24/25/28/29/32/36/40, 5/16/17/19/30/32/34/35/37/39, 10/23/24/25/27/28/31/32/36/40, 8/17/24/30/31/32/34/35/37/39, 22/25/27/28/29/30/31/32/36/40, 27/28/29/30/31/32/34/35/37/39 and 29/30/31/32/34/35/36/37/39/40, of which only the last is forced, because it is the only ten distinct permitted values that foot to three hundred and forty-three.** **no barred numeral in any price, over-figure or printed total, and the figures 14, 20, 26, 33, 38 and 41 appear nowhere in the block's money at all.** **The thank family is 34 and every one of the 34 is a negation or a plain statement that a person was not thanked, and the ten occurrences of a Crown officer saying thank you at a gate were rewritten out of the block before the first count and the substance of them was kept as that it was very good of her.** The word `brother` is at 0 in this block, because `chapter-0654.md` already spends the volume's cap of four, and `sister` is at 0 across the volume. **The words *stage*, *ledger* and *qualification* are at 0 across the ten files, no System panel is printed, the word *failed* and its whole family are at 0, and `Edric Hunn` is at 0 and the name at the top of the sheet is called the Marshal's hand.**

## The five wrong rows in the printed calendar table, all five named, and the highest number in this file is now 1561

1561. **THE PRINTED CALENDAR TABLE AT `outline/volume-13.md` §17 IS WRONG IN FIVE ROWS AND THIS PASS FOUND ALL FIVE, AND THEY ARE 2106, 2136, 2166, 2196 AND 2256.** The table's own header says it is wrong in five rows and nobody had ever said which five. **On the chain this project actually runs, which is built forward from the single anchor of day 930, the twenty-fifth of Mudmonth YR 318, a Sunday, on the carried thirty-day month, and which reconciles against all twenty datelines of Volume 14 written so far: day 2106 is a Sunday and the table prints a Monday; day 2136 is a Tuesday and the table prints a Wednesday; day 2166 is a Thursday and the table prints a Saturday; day 2196 is a Saturday and the table prints a Monday; and day 2256 is a Wednesday and the table prints a Saturday.** Every other row the table prints, including **2040, 2041, 2046, 2076 and 2310**, is right. **The month starts are the reliable half of that table and the weekdays are the broken half**, which is consistent with a table that was filled in down the month column and had its weekday column carried across by hand, and it means a writer who copies a month boundary out of it will be right and a writer who copies a weekday out of it has a one-in-three chance. **No Volume 14 writer may read a weekday off that table. The ten datelines of Batch 0002 are at `state/continuity.md` 1551 and they all reconcile, and those are the figures a successor should check against.** Block 0003's own frame is at `state/open-threads.md` 557 and in its prompt, and it now carries the corrected Embermonth the first as a Thursday and a market morning and the corrected Wolfmonth the first as a Saturday and a market morning.

## The review repair over Volume 14 Batch 0002, items 1562 to 1566, and the highest number in this file is now 1566

**A review was run over the finished block before the state files were closed. It returned fourteen blockers, eleven majors and a long list of minors. All fourteen blockers and nine of the eleven majors are repaired in place. No chapter was restarted, no plot beat was moved, no card was dropped, and the ten money totals did not change.**

1562. **The fourteen blockers, and every one of them was real, and five of them were arithmetic or a date.** **One, the counted sum in `chapter-0668.md` did not foot: nine households giving fourpence, twopence and a penny came to seventeen pence and the chapter printed thirty-one.** It now foots on the page and the reader is shown the footing, which is twenty-two, six and three. **Two, the badge-man's standing was thirty-one years in nine places and `chapter-0668.md` said nine.** **Three, `chapter-0667.md` had the missing lad reaching the top of the made road an hour before the barrow went up the lane, and the seventh hour is now the hour the crew turned off, and the sentence in which the man with the crew corrects himself has been added, which is the best thing the repair did.** **Four, the same chapter said the barrow came down in the dark against its own clock and now says that the ninth hour is dark by the end of Harvestmonth, and the two state files that had inherited the phrase have been corrected.** **Five, `chapter-0666.md` had the badge-man's month of silence declared on the seventh of Harvestmonth, when he declared it on the first, and four days, when thirteen had gone by.** **Six, the line a serjeant entered in Chapter 0670 read the thirteenth of Fallowmonth on the day the question was asked, and the question was asked on the twelfth, which is the Saturday, and the entry now reads the twelfth.** **Seven, Chapter 0670 said the first of a month was three days off and it is eighteen.** **Eight, the same sentence said nine hours where it meant one hour.** **Nine, `chapter-0666.md` had the party sleeping in a barn at the ferry stone, which is four miles from the market town, while standing at a gate two days off, and the copyist's errand attributed to her out loud in that market on the twenty-eighth of Rainmonth, which is a scene that belongs to Maud Trenchard in another county; the errand is now hers and it is given to her at a gate in the market town on the second of Fallowmonth by a woman of about fifty-six with a bag, in about nine words, on her way home.** **Ten, eleven, twelve, thirteen and fourteen are the finding's age in Chapters 0661, 0665, 0667 and 0669 and in the two chapters that claim a count of the questions Aren Kest has asked; the finding now dates from the eighth of Longlight, which is the only datable origin the manuscript gives, and the ages are six weeks, eight weeks and about ten weeks, and the ordinal counts are gone.**

1563. **Two structural majors are repaired and they are the two the review put first and second.** **The block's engine had been spent a chapter early: Chapter 0661 walked all three clauses one by one and reached the same place Chapter 0662 reaches in the better sentence and the one the letter earns, so Chapter 0661's speech has been replaced with the badge-man declining to read the three clauses a second time, a true nothing, and the reason a rule in this street is worth nothing to a woman four days off until somebody in her own county has been asked something, and the chapter now ends on the first hand report it was written to carry.** **And the block closed on a rerun of Chapter 0668's closing beat, the mason and the top course and the mallet, which meant the last page of the block was a page from two chapters earlier. It has been rebuilt. The block now ends half a mile up the road, at a gate where the water comes over the crown, with the woman of about fifty putting her lamp down on the dry side, standing in four inches of water, and saying out loud that there has never been a name on that road and that there is one now and that the difference is that in about four years a man will be able to say she had been told and she will not be able to say she had not.** The mallet and the fourth course are untouched where they were and appear nowhere in the last page.

1564. **The other seven majors that are repaired, one line each.** **The woman of about thirty-six's second delivery of the second hole in Chapter 0666 is cut, because Chapter 0664 gave it and giving it twice in nine days to the same people is not a speech, it is a habit; she is now offered the sentence and declines it out loud, and the badge-man's not saying it is what the chapter is about.** **The four-and-five census had broken arithmetically in two places, in Chapters 0665 and 0668, and both are fixed, and the badge-man's stock phrase in the voice he uses is cut to two occurrences out of five and the other three are written out in the particular way each of those three men says a thing.** **There were three women of about forty-four in the block; the woman who walks two days in the wet is now about thirty-nine and the woman at the gate in the second county is now about fifty-eight, and the woman who keeps a crossing four hundred miles off keeps forty-four, and about thirty-six, thirty-eight, twenty-nine, fifty, fifty-six and fifty-eight are all distinct.** **The man of about thirty-one who carries the letter in Chapter 0662 is now about thirty-seven, because the man of about thirty-one in Chapter 0666 is the officer with the small book and two adjacent chapters are the wrong place to spend the same age on two different men.** **The man of about forty-four who appears once in Chapter 0663 with no want has been given one, which is that he came to see whether the quarter sheet on the boards says what a man in a livery read out to him at a gate in his own county, and that it does not, and that he has nine days of working out what to do with that.** **Two bare paragraph-openers of nobody thanked in Chapters 0662 and 0667 are rewritten so that four of the ten chapters do not open a paragraph with the phrase.**

1565. **The one thing the review did not get right, recorded because a successor will meet it and should not be misled.** It reported `chapter-0661.md`'s "six weeks" for the finding as an error against `chapter-0660.md`'s "three weeks and two days." **Six weeks is right and `chapter-0660.md` is the figure that does not reconcile, and this pass did not repair it, because it is a closed block's figure and the standing rule is that a successor does not repair a closed chapter on a hunch.** **What this pass did do is re-anchor Batch 0002 on the only datable origin the manuscript gives, which is the eighth of Longlight, and record the arithmetic here so that the next writer does not have to choose between two inherited figures.** Three weeks and two days before the second of Rainmonth is the twenty-fourth of Longlight's week, and the eighth of Longlight is the day Tovan Kest says you are taking the level of the run and not of the road, which is the day the finding is on the page.

1566. **What the review confirmed independently and a successor need not check.** All ten money columns foot four ways with the conversion checked separately from the sum, and every barred numeral is absent from every price, every over-figure and every printed total. Every constraint in the writer's brief holds: zero for the antagonist's name, zero for the kin-terms, zero for stage, ledger, qualification, mechanism, custodian and charter, zero for the whole fail family, zero System panels, zero meta-language, zero for a figure for the column, and neither hole in the arrangement is called small anywhere, the six occurrences of small thing being the established one true small thing and four explicit denials that a hole is one. Exact duplicate paragraphs at twelve words or more are 0 against the 660 prior files and 1 inside the block, and the one is the letter read twice. The four recurring local figures are individuated and the badge-man, who was the review's exception, is better embodied after the repair than before it. **The review's own top two passages are the ninth of nine reasons in `chapter-0667.md` and the mason owning his own mistake in his own trade in `chapter-0668.md`, and neither was touched.**

## Volume 14 Batch 0003 canon, items 1567 to 1578, and the measurement at 1579

**This section is written by the phase that wrote Volume 14 Batch 0003 and wrote its block, and `outline/volume-14.md` §8 Block 3 is the plan it was written against and is binding. `state/open-threads.md` items 557 to 574 were Batch 0003's whole instruction and item 570 was the list of what it must not do. Nothing above is renumbered and nothing above is resolved.**

1567. **THE FRAME BATCH 0003 WAS WRITTEN TO, DERIVED AND NOT COPIED, AND THE TEN DAYS.** The single anchor is **day 930, the twenty-fifth of Mudmonth YR 318, a Sunday**, on the carried thirty-day month, and the chain is built forward from **day 2040, the twenty-fifth of Greenmonth YR 321, a Thursday and a market morning**, which four separate places state, with the day-of-month half and the weekday half derived separately. **Block 0003 is days 2149 to 2202 inclusive, fifty-four days, that is the fourteenth of Fallowmonth YR 321 to the seventh of Wolfmonth YR 321, and every day of it is YR 321, because the year does not turn until day 2341.** Month starts inside the block are **the first of Embermonth at day 2166 and the first of Wolfmonth at day 2196, and the first of Embermonth is a Thursday and a market morning and the first of Wolfmonth is a Saturday and a market morning.** **The ten chapter days are 2154, 2159, 2164, 2166, 2173, 2175, 2178, 2181, 2182 and 2196.** Their datelines in order: **Fallowmonth the nineteenth, a Saturday and a market morning; Fallowmonth the twenty-fourth, a Thursday and a market morning; Fallowmonth the twenty-ninth, a Tuesday and not a market morning, the room shut; Embermonth the first, a Thursday and a market morning and the first of a month; Embermonth the eighth, a Thursday and a market morning; Embermonth the tenth, a Saturday and a market morning; Embermonth the thirteenth, a Tuesday and not a market morning, the room shut; Embermonth the sixteenth, a Friday and not a market morning, the room shut; Embermonth the seventeenth, a Saturday and a market morning; Wolfmonth the first, a Saturday and a market morning and the first of a month.** **Seven are market mornings and three are not, the three being a Tuesday, a Tuesday and a Friday, and the room over the weigh-house is shut on all three and each of those three chapters says so in the dateline. Two are the first of a month. The printed table at `outline/volume-13.md` §17 was not read for any part of this derivation.**

1568. **THE BLOCK'S DECISION, IN FULL, because a successor must not soften it or complete it.** On the nineteenth of Fallowmonth, in a market street, **Salla Dunnock, 38**, of the coast of returning roads, standing in her own place and named as a return, said that the seven roads were to be asked one question out loud and that one of four people had to go up there to say it. **The four are a man of about fifty-eight with a badge and no head, a man of about thirty-four with a sheet in his coat, a woman of about thirty-six who keeps a road in the county where a list of names was burned, and Salla Dunnock.** **Three of the four said no out loud in that street.** The reason none of them will go is **that there is no head in nine counties with which to send anybody, and a thing that sends a person is a head, and the badge-man has said nine times that he will not make one.** **Nobody in that street had agreed with her decision before she made it and nobody agreed with it afterwards**, and the badge-man said the true thing about it in nine words and did not try to stop her. **She said she would get up on the Monday and walk out up the made road with nine days of road in front of her, and that nobody in nine counties would have sent her.** Nobody thanked anybody.

1569. **THE UNITED FRONT, IN FULL, because it is wrong and a successor must be able to find out what it said.** **Nine hundred words in nine numbered clauses, written by Aren Kest at a settle in a full inn on the twenty-ninth of Fallowmonth, and it is on the page.** The seven roads are named in plain terms: **the ferry city, the county where a list of names was burned, the waterworks country, the coast of returning roads, the fruit valley, the glass country, and the small one.** **It gives no figure of men, horses or households and no distance and it puts nothing into any return, roll or sheet. It names no road.** Its sixth clause is the wrong one: **for the length of the emergency, each of the seven roads accepts the authority of the Crown in the keeping of its own crossing, in writing, and the writing is held by the person named on the road's side and by nobody in this county.** Its seventh clause is **that the acceptance may be ended by the person named on the road's side at any hour, without notice and without a reason given.** Its ninth clause gives the reason and **the reason is good**, being that a road that has not put its name to a paper cannot be found, and a thing that is not in the taking cannot be argued with by anybody including the people in it. **He gave it to nobody: not to the woman who keeps a room, not to the copyist, and not to the man with a badge, and the reason he gave out loud for the last of those is that a man with a badge who carries nine hundred words is a head.** Nobody was thanked and nobody was asked to sign it.

1570. **THE OBJECTION, THE ANSWER, AND THE THING THE BADGE-MAN SAID ABOUT BOTH, because the whole of the block turns on the second of them.** In a market street on the first of Embermonth, **a woman of about thirty-six who agrees with eight of the nine clauses gave the number of the one she does not, which is the sixth, and said it cannot be complied with by a road that does not take orders**, because to accept an order a road has to have somebody in it who gives orders and there is nobody at the end of any of the seven. **The answer, given by Aren Kest, is that the road is not asked to give an order and a person is asked for a name and the paper goes to the person and there is nobody in the chain.** **And then the badge-man said the true thing about it in nine words, and it is the load-bearing sentence of the block: a person who can be found afterwards is what the burden has been waiting four and a half years for, and this county has just written nine hundred words handing it over.** Nobody thanked anybody and nobody in that street answered it.

1571. **THE SECOND HALF OF THE STANDING DEFECT PAID, AND IT PAID TWICE, IN FULL, because the figure must not move and the question must not be answered again.** On the eighth of Embermonth a printed list of about thirty counties in order went onto the boards of the market town and was read out loud, and **against each county there is one road written out in full, and against the fourth county down there is a road four miles long with a stone in the mud at the low end and a milestone at the top.** At the top of it, in a hand that is not the printed hand of the body, **is the antagonist's name, printed once in this block and read out twice in one market street, and every other reference to it in the block is the words *that name* or *the Marshal's hand*.** **The captain's forward party has been doing this for nine days, one road in one county at a time, off the same list, with a chain on two posts and about four men and no written order to hold anything, and nobody in nine counties has standing to say a road is his.** `Halvard Rees` said three things and the third is that a list does not let him be wrong about anything, and that he has not read the last twenty-one counties on it. **The badge-man said the interval between the twenty-fifth of Greenmonth and the sixteenth of Longlight was twenty-one days and that he had said a longer figure and was wrong by about three months and about nine days, and he did not move the figure and did not state the longer figure.** **He said out loud that it was a kindness. A man of about thirty-three asked whether being right early was a kindness, and the badge-man did not answer it, and the woman of about fifty answered it: it was a kindness and it was to him, the twenty-one days went on a rule about a road being shut, that rule has taken a horse and a barrow and not one man with a paper, a kindness is a thing you do to a person, and if he had given the longer figure they would have spent the longer figure on the same rule and he will never know that.** **Nobody thanked her and the answer stands and is not withdrawn anywhere in the block.**

1572. **THE REVERSAL, IN FULL, because it is four sentences and a successor must not have them second-hand.** In the hired room over a saddler's on the tenth of Embermonth, **Sera Quen, about thirty-four, a ferrywright who keeps a crossing and the approaches to it, of a city four hundred miles off that was never severed, walked nine days into a room with about nine people in it and stood up and said four sentences.** They are: **that the sixth clause wants seven roads to accept the authority of the Crown in the keeping of their own crossings for the length of the emergency, in writing, with a named person on each side; that a road cannot be made to do a thing because there is nobody at the end of a road to be told anything, so a road cannot be asked whether it has been told and cannot answer; that the day one of the seven roads puts a name on the paper, a man will have a name to say at a gate for the rest of his life and the road will not be able to open its mouth, and that is not a new thing in this world and is what a Sundering is; and that four and a half years have been built on a road not having a name on it and nine hundred words put one on every road in nine counties, and she is not going to be one of the nine people in that room who agrees to it.** **The protagonist answered her question at length before she spoke and was wrong and did not know he was wrong. He said out loud that he was not going to ask her to say it again and was not going to ask her to put it another way. She said one further sentence and it was not softening: that he did not write it to be wrong, and that she had walked nine days to say it in a room and not in a street.** She went back up the made road on the Wednesday of the eighteenth and refused a copy of the nine hundred words and was not argued with. **`Sera Quen` appears in Chapter 0676 and in no other of the ten files, and she is an established return from Volume 01 and not a name-budget spend.**

1573. **THE COST OF THE BLOCK, IN FULL, AND IT IS A PERSON.** **About nine tons of cut stone stand in the low approach to a crossing nine miles off in the market town's own county, put there by a Crown crew on the fourth of Fallowmonth at about the ninth hour to stand a sill on, and nobody has come back for it and there is nothing to lift.** Nine households at that crossing have been going round by the lane since the fourth of Fallowmonth and **four of them have stopped going round and have stopped going at all**, and about four households at that crossing go over a bank in about nine minutes every time a loaded cart crosses and **none of them has said no to anything, because there has never been a thing to say no to.** **A woman of about forty-four who keeps that crossing with the man who keeps it with her walked nine miles on foot on the thirteenth of Embermonth to say no to the sixth clause before she was asked it.** **She had already said no to one Crown paper once, on the sixth of Fallowmonth, and the man wrote it down and read it back.** Her reason is **a crossing, and a Tuesday after that, and the man who keeps that crossing with her going down on foot in the rain and asking the officer with the small book whether the district would require the stone taken away, and being told no, politely, and having it written down and read back, and not being able to put it down since.** **She said she would say yes to a thing that has nobody's name on it and has not got one and is not going to make one up on a Tuesday.** **She is not a villain and she did not raise her voice once. The badge-man said in nine words that a thing that cannot take orders cannot sign anything and that it is not a hole. The man of about thirty-four worked out out loud that nine counties has written a plan that cannot be signed and that there is no clause about it. The woman of about forty-four and Aren Kest do not speak again anywhere in this block.** **She is not the woman of about forty-four who keeps a crossing four hundred miles off in `chapter-0669.md`, and a successor must keep the two apart.**

1574. **THE SMALLEST VERSION OF A THING, IN FULL, because it is the answer and it costs somebody.** On the sixteenth of Embermonth, on a lane in a county two days off, **a man of about thirty-one who is not the man who marked the ford put a board on the inside of nine gates in about four minutes each, describing each gate by its fields and elms and pond, putting the day and his own name and one line saying that he asked which place or road was meant and that the person at the gate said she had not been told, and reading it back to each of them.** **At the ninth gate, four feet before he reached it, the woman of about thirty-nine came out of her own gate and said no before he asked.** **He said out loud that he had got nothing to put in the book, because there is no line on his page for a person who has said no to a question nobody asked, and he wrote it on the back of the page for that gate and under the board where a person could find it, and not in the small book.** **She said the answer to the question she had walked two days in the wet to ask in Rainmonth is that he has the right, he is going to ask, and the answer is no, and she had said it before he asked it.** **She said she cannot promise to say it a second time and can promise that about nine people on that lane will know somebody said it first.** Nobody thanked anybody and somebody is in the rain at the end of it. **This is the question `chapter-0661.md` put on the page and the copyist could not answer, and it is answered on the page here by a person saying no rather than by an authority answering, and no chapter says the question is settled.**

1575. **THE SECOND MOUTH AND THE SECOND COST, IN FULL, because they are both restated and the difference between this block and Block 0004 is load-bearing.** On the seventeenth of Embermonth **a man of about thirty-four with a satchel had walked four of the seven roads on his own account and on his own time and had asked four of them the sixth clause. Two wrote back and two sent nothing. The two that sent nothing are the waterworks country and the glass country, and they are NOT the two the column is standing on, which is the made road to the coast and the four-mile road at the ford with a chain on it, and a woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a horse says that difference out loud in a market street.** **A successor must not confuse this with Block 0004, where the two that send nothing are the two the column is standing on.** **The second cost of the volume is said out loud again in a different mouth:** a road that has decided something is not a return and is not a roll and is not a sheet going out to a district, and there is no head for it in nine counties, and the copyist said the same thing in a room over a saddler's on the tenth and in four markets before that. **Nine households' money was counted at the boards for four roads walked to: eighteen, eleven, eleven, seven, four, four, four and nine, which is seventy pence, written on the back of a sheet and not in anything else, and the man of about thirty-four said his own house gave nothing before anybody read it out and was not argued with.** The three series, the band and the roll are unchanged and no chapter merges them or derives one from another.

1576. **THE BLOCK'S ORDINARY HALF, IN FULL, because it is long and it is the thing and it is not a summary.** On the seventeenth of Embermonth, at about the fourth hour, **about nine people turned out at a barrel drain on a mile and a half of lane that nobody ordered and that is not the market town's business, and it was the fifth time in about four years.** About three inches of silt, about two of it a lime crust gone hard in a night, came out in pieces; a chain on a bar went through the mouth of the cut and **four of the nine could not lift it at the second pull and five could, and the man of about fifty said out loud that that was the correct proportion.** The boy of about seventeen was on the bucket end from the fourth hour and counted **a hundred and forty trips** out loud. **The man of about fifty-one who owns the field on the low side of the cut said out loud that he has been coming on Fridays since the middle of Rainmonth for nothing and asked that it be said in a market street and not in a lane, and the woman of about thirty-eight told him he would not be thanked for it and would not be argued with and said she had said it to be unkind, because a thing nobody has been thanked for and nobody has been argued with is a thing that cannot be given up.** **The barrel came clear and the water found it and went down the ditch, and the man of about fifty-one went out into the field with a lamp and found a new watercourse about four feet long in a corner of it.** **Nobody thanked anybody.** **This is a barrow drain on a lane and it is NOT the culvert at the market town's own gate, the line of stone on the bank and the mallet belong to `chapter-0668.md` and appear nowhere in this block, and the mile and a half is not the road that went quiet in Thawmonth.**

1577. **THE CLOSE OF THE BLOCK, IN FULL, because three things stand and are not repaired.** On the first of Wolfmonth, a first of a month, **the nine hundred words are on a table in the hired room over a saddler's under a cloth, which is not a thing anybody in nine counties has ever done to a paper, and nobody put the cloth there and nobody has been able to establish who did and nobody has asked.** Two people tried to take a copy and both were refused and neither was argued with, and **the copyist did not read the small road's letter for a third time and said nine words about it out loud, which are that it stands and that it is a decision and not a promise broken.** **The badge-man said the standing defect paid, in nine words, and did not move the figure, and the answer to whether being right early was a kindness stands where the woman of about fifty gave it.** **Aren Kest said out loud that he is not taking the plan back, and his reason is that the fifth, sixth and seventh clauses are the same three clauses about what goes into a book and what a person is called and about four of the nine people in that street know those three are the best three anybody in this county has written, and a county that takes back the three because the sixth is wrong has learned to take back anything.** **And he said he is not going to change the sixth, because the only person in nine counties who has ever checked those nine hundred words lives four hundred miles off and will not be here in a month.** **And the badge-man said then somebody local has to check it and there is not one of those either, and that is the true thing about the gap in a thing they had just agreed was not a hole.** **The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps the room was on the thirteen stairs with the register shut and did not come down, and she and Aren Kest did not speak, and it is said on the page that nine people noticed and about five did not, and nothing was mended.** **And the small concrete local decision is not the protagonist's: a woman of about fifty-nine who keeps the second gate from the top of the mile and a half, who gave a bay gelding of about nine hands to four men in a livery on the twenty-second of Rainmonth and said afterwards that she would have said no and did not get as far as it, and who has said out loud that she does not know what to do with a person who asks, decides to go and ask somebody one question. Nobody sent her. She put nine pounds of lime in a hole at the top of the lane, which nobody in nine counties knows about and which is not in any series. She went down the lane in the rain at about the sixth hour to the top house and did not knock for about as long as it takes a carter to get a load round and said out loud that she was about fifty-nine and that she kept the second gate from the top and that she had not come about a horse. Then she knocked.** Nobody thanked anybody.

1578. **THE PEOPLE THIS BLOCK ADDED, BY AGE AND TRADE, because a successor must not double them up.** **A chandler of about thirty-three** at the low end of the market street's boards, who counts money out loud and who said the thing cannot be asked. **A man of about twenty-nine who keeps the inn two doors up**, who said out loud that the hearing was the wrong size for a thing for the first time and who has a room where a person can sit six hours and be asked nothing, and who said he is not a road and is not one of the seven. **A man of about thirty-three who sells cloth three doors up**, who asked whether being right early was a kindness. **A man of about twenty-nine who keeps a horse at the top of the market street**, who had said nothing at the boards for about a year and who says the difference between the two roads that sent nothing and the two the column is on. **A man of about forty-three who keeps a yard four streets up the market street**, who has been going over nine tons of stone in nine minutes a time for nine weeks. **A man of about forty-four who sells nails at the high end of the boards**, who argued about the yard opposite and was not thanked for it. **A man of about forty-seven who sells wool two streets off**, who understood every word and got none of it. **A man of about forty-three who reads four clauses and points at one.** **A carter of about forty-one**, who priced himself at four pence a day and wants his cart back whatever happens. **A woman of about fifty-seven with a basket and a stick** in a barn on the made road, who has done forty runs. **A man of about seventy at the fifth gate** who said he had never been told one thing by anybody in a coat. **A woman of about forty-one who keeps a chandler's shop** and read nine lines of the plan standing up and pointed at the sixth clause and said nothing. **A woman of about fifty-nine who keeps the second gate from the top of the mile and a half**, who decided to go and ask one question. **A woman of about thirty-one who keeps the smith's house at the junction**, who has a want of her own and who told the woman of about fifty-nine that she would come to be watched. **A woman of about thirty-eight off the coast of returning roads** with a carter, who has a name already spent and is not a spend here. **And two figures of about thirty-eight stand in this block and a successor should keep them apart: the woman of about thirty-eight who keeps the tally at a post on the coast of returning roads, and the woman of about thirty-eight with a bar who worked the mile and a half in `chapter-0665.md` and is on the cut in `chapter-0679.md`.** 
1579. **THE MEASUREMENTS, RECOUNTED ON THE TEN FILES AS THEY STAND, WITH THE TWO BASES NAMED SEPARATELY.** **BASIS ONE, the file total: a plain whitespace split with the title line INCLUDED, each file counted on its own and the ten added. The block IS 33,246**, per chapter **3,231 / 3,188 / 3,425 / 3,208 / 3,596 / 3,648 / 3,204 / 3,035 / 3,230 / 3,481**, and a concatenated `cat` of the ten returns 33,246 and every file ends in a newline, so the two bases agree. **BASIS TWO, the scene body: everything above the paragraph that opens the cost coda, which is the file total less the cost line less the money column paragraph, and which THEREFORE INCLUDES THE TITLE LINE AND THE DATELINE, so a body is not a scene body. The bodies ARE 2,948 / 2,905 / 3,138 / 2,916 / 3,349 / 3,360 / 2,954 / 2,748 / 2,921 / 3,197**, minimum 2,748 at `chapter-0678.md`. Codas are **283 / 283 / 287 / 292 / 247 / 288 / 250 / 287 / 309 / 284**. **`about` IS 24.45 to 28.17 per 1,000** over the ten, and the mean is **3,325 a chapter**, against Volume 13's five blocks at a mean of 2,679 and against this volume's two earlier blocks at 3,185 and 3,404. **Zero em dashes, zero en dashes, zero semicolons, zero exclamation marks, zero single asterisks and therefore no italics, zero straight double quotes and zero curly apostrophes**, curly doubles balanced in every file, per-line bold parity even on every line, and **2 / 1 / 1 / 1 / 1 / 2 / 1 / 1 / 1 / 1 question marks, thirteen in all, with no chapter at zero.** `Aren Kest` is in all ten. **Exact duplicate paragraphs at twelve words or more are 0 against the 670 prior files and 0 inside the block.** **The longest shared eight-word run against the 670 prior files is 23 windows, which is 30 words, at `chapter-0672.md`, and the next four are 20, 18, 18 and 17**, all of them house recurrences, **and the longest shared run inside the block is 23 windows, at `chapter-0672.md` against `chapter-0678.md`, and it is the house's own census sentence and not a reprise.** **MONEY, with the extractor first and in the standing order. The scope is all 670 chapter files before this block, read whole and not sliced to the last two paragraphs. Extractor one, the printed total, clause-anchored on `is <spelled number> pence.` and taken at a parsed value of 60 or above, returns 198 instances and 181 distinct. Extractor two, the restated total inside `and <spelled number> pence is <spelled number> marks and <spelled number> over`, returns 140 instances and 135 distinct, and under this extractor IS A STRICT SUBSET of extractor one, which is a difference from the extractor and scope printed at 1560 and is recorded here rather than reconciled. The union is 181 distinct. The four barred numerals appear nowhere in it, so the free set is 181 of 181. Column count: ten. Collision result: ZERO. 190 of the 670 prior files carry a printed total at or above 60 under this extractor.** The ten totals are **239, 257, 265, 275, 284, 290, 297, 303, 306 and 312**, ten distinct, ascending, each footing four ways, ten items a column, ten distinct prices a column from 1 to 41, **all ten tails distinct so that no two columns end on the same two items at the same two prices, the lowest price in the block is 11, and no barred numeral is in any price, over-figure or printed total.** **THE THANK FAMILY IS 83 AND EVERY ONE OF THE 83 IS A NEGATION OR A PLAIN STATEMENT THAT A PERSON WAS NOT THANKED.** The word `brother` and the word `sister` are at 0 across the ten files. The words *stage*, *ledger*, *qualification*, *mechanism*, *custodian* and *charter* are at 0, no System panel is printed, and **the whole *fail* family is at 0.** `Edric Hunn` is printed once and `Sera Quen` once per chapter in one chapter only. **AND THE MANUSCRIPT IS 1,905,179 WORDS IN SIX HUNDRED AND EIGHTY CHAPTERS, Volumes 01 to 13 are 1,805,098 in six hundred and fifty and did not move, and Volume 14 is 100,081 in thirty at a mean of 3,336.**
1580. **THE FRAME BATCH 0004 WAS WRITTEN TO, DERIVED AND NOT COPIED, AND THE TEN DAYS.** The single anchor is **day 930, the twenty-fifth of Mudmonth YR 318, a Sunday**, on the carried thirty-day month, and the chain is built forward from **day 2040, the twenty-fifth of Greenmonth YR 321, a Thursday and a market morning**, which four separate places state, with the day-of-month half and the weekday half derived separately and never from one another. **Block 0004 is days 2203 to 2256 inclusive, fifty-four days, that is the eighth of Wolfmonth YR 321 to the first of Hearthmonth YR 321, and every day of it is YR 321, because the year does not turn until day 2341.** Month starts inside the block are **the first of Frostmonth at day 2226, a Monday and not a market morning, and the first of Hearthmonth at day 2256, a Wednesday and not a market morning, and 2256 is the last day of the block.** **The ten chapter days are 2203, 2208, 2211, 2215, 2222, 2226, 2231, 2236, 2238 and 2256.** Their datelines in order: **Wolfmonth the eighth, a Saturday and a market morning; Wolfmonth the thirteenth, a Thursday and a market morning; Wolfmonth the sixteenth, a Sunday and not a market morning, the room shut; Wolfmonth the twentieth, a Thursday and a market morning; Wolfmonth the twenty-seventh, a Thursday and a market morning; Frostmonth the first, a Monday and not a market morning and the first of a month, the room shut; Frostmonth the sixth, a Saturday and a market morning; Frostmonth the eleventh, a Thursday and a market morning; Frostmonth the thirteenth, a Saturday and a market morning; Hearthmonth the first, a Wednesday and not a market morning and the first of a month, the room shut.** **Seven are market mornings and three are not, the three being a Sunday, a Monday and a Wednesday, and the room over the weigh-house is shut on all three and each of those three chapters says so in the dateline. Two are the first of a month, and neither of the two is a market morning. There are fifteen market mornings in the fifty-four days of the block.** The day before the block, day 2202, is Wolfmonth the seventh, a Friday, which is the day Chapter 0680 was written on. **The printed table at `outline/volume-13.md` §17 was not read for any part of this derivation.**

1581. **THE BLOCK'S FINDING, IN FULL, because a successor must not soften it, complete it or answer it.** On the first of Hearthmonth, a Wednesday, in a market street with no market in it and about four people at the boards, **a man of about thirty-four with a satchel said that nine counties has got about nine people and no document, and that the two words for that are either coalition or nothing, and that he was not going to say either of them in a market street and was not going to say the second one in this county at all.** His reason, in full and on the page: **a thing with no document cannot be signed and cannot be given away and cannot be taken off anybody, and that is not a failure of the arrangement, and he had been looking for something wrong with the arrangement for about five weeks and there is not one thing, and the worst thing about the arrangement is that it cannot be given away, and he spent two months in Fallowmonth writing nine hundred words trying to give it away.** Nobody thanked him and nothing was decided and it is not resolved.

1582. **THE COALITION THAT HAS NO CHARTER, AS THE BLOCK PUT IT ON THE PAGE, AND IT IS RENDERED IN THE MANUSCRIPT'S OWN WORD.** There is no paper and nobody has standing to say what it is. **The badge-man said in nine words on the eighth of Wolfmonth and again in Chapter 0682 that a thing with no head is not a smaller thing, it is a harder thing to get at, and that nobody in this county is going to call it anything, because you do not name a thing to get at it, you name a thing so that it can be got at, by the people in it and by the people who want it.** The word *charter* is at 0 across the ten files and the word *custodian* is at 0, and the manuscript's word for the thing is a thing with no document and no name at the top of it. **A successor may name it if a card says so and may not otherwise.**

1583. **THE THREE NUMBERS, IN FULL, because a successor must not change them and must not derive from them.** On the eighth of Wolfmonth the copyist of about twenty-six gave three figures out loud at the boards in the flat way: **about four people in nine counties have been asked things this year and about four of them are the only people in nine counties who have been asked anything; about nine people have come to this street ready to say no; and about four of those nine are the only ones of the nine who could carry anything at all, and by carry anything is meant walking four days up a made road and standing in front of a person and saying one question and coming back.** **The hard fact, given in the same market street, is that the four who could carry are the four who said no on the nineteenth of Fallowmonth, all four, in about nine seconds each, in front of about nine people.** All four were asked in the open on the eighth of Wolfmonth, one at a time, and all four said no in about four seconds, and the standing reason is that a person who carries a thing for nine counties is a head. **The four of them are a woman of about thirty-six who keeps a road in the county where a list of names was burned, the man of about thirty-four with a satchel, a woman of about thirty-eight who keeps a tally at a post on the coast of returning roads, and a man of about forty-four who keeps a wain and a team of four.**

1584. **THE TENTH QUESTION, IN FULL, because a successor must not fill it and must not rule anything over it.** On the sixteenth of Wolfmonth, a Sunday, in the room over the weigh-house with the door shut behind its own door, the copyist of about twenty-six **ruled a tenth question and ruled nothing else.** It is on the page and it is: **of a thing with no head, and of a road that will not be named, what may be said to have been decided, and by whom it may be brought to.** **The register now holds ten questions and no finding under any of the ten, and a fourth ruled head over a place where a finding goes that is empty and has been empty for about eleven months, and the copyist has said out loud, in three months and in two rooms, that she is not going to rule a fifth head and that neither is anybody else.** The day-book stands at **eleven entries.** The badge-man's objection and her refusal of it are on the page: a question with no head over it will be read as a finding in about four years, and **it will be a question because she wrote a question, and a book does not say, and a person who decides that about four and a half years of this county was a county that put down what it knew can live with it.** The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps that room **says that the fourth head ought not to be there, is right about it, has said it twice in about four and a half years, and is refused in four words, that it is not hers to rule, and is not argued with.**

1585. **THE SECOND MOUTH AND THE DIFFERENCE FROM THE PREVIOUS BLOCK, IN FULL, BECAUSE THE DIFFERENCE IS LOAD-BEARING AND A SUCCESSOR MUST NOT CONFUSE IT.** On the thirteenth of Wolfmonth a man of about thirty-four with a satchel said he had asked four roads **a different question, about a boy, and not the sixth clause**, on his own account and on his own time, having gone out on the Monday and come back on the Wednesday. **Two wrote and two sent nothing. THE TWO THAT SENT NOTHING ARE THE COAST ROAD AND THE FOUR MILES AT THE FORD, AND THEY ARE THE TWO THE COLUMN IS STANDING ON, AND THAT IS THE DIFFERENCE FROM BATCH 0003, WHERE THE TWO THAT SENT NOTHING WERE THE WATERWORKS COUNTRY AND THE GLASS COUNTRY AND WERE NOT THE TWO THE COLUMN WAS ON.** The difference was said out loud in a market street by **a woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a horse at the top of the market street, who is not the protagonist, and who said the shape has turned over: a road that will not answer was a road nobody had touched and is now a road with four men on it, and when it is read off a list there will be no moment and no name and nobody in nine counties can say a word about it, because the only people who could say a word about it are the two who are not writing.** A road that has written is not a return and is not a roll and is not a sheet going out to a district, **and the third series cannot carry the answer, said by the copyist in a market street in a fourth form, and the fact that a road answered goes into nothing on Wednesday and is still in nothing on Thursday, and in about four years a man in a room is going to ask this county what the seven roads have said and the true answer is that this county does not know, and it will not be lie.** The two sheets that came back were from the county where a list of names was burned, four lines, and the fruit valley, nine lines in two hands, **and the copyist read the first four lines of the second one and refused in the open to read the other five in a market street.** The man of about thirty-four wrote the day and the two roads on the back of a sheet and refused to put it in a book.

1586. **THE ROAD THAT REFUSES TO NAME A WITNESS, IN FULL, AND IT IS THE BLOCK'S SENTENCE.** On the twentieth of Wolfmonth **a man of about fifty-two who keeps four miles of made road under contract for about nineteen years and hires about nine men to it in Frostmonth came up the market street on foot with about nine of his men behind him at an agreed distance and said he will not give the witness clause a name.** His grounds, in order: the clause wants somebody named by the people who work the road and he has nine men on that road and about four of them since the middle of Rainmonth; the first no was his own, this one is on behalf of nine men, and he has asked none of them, because if he asks them then he is a man who asks, and he has been a man who asks for about nineteen years and does not want to find out what he is when a thing is on a page. **And then the sentence, which nobody in nine counties has softened and which the badge-man made the block's by saying it twice out loud in a market street in the man's own words: a shut road has to be proposed by somebody, a burden only falls on a man who proposed, that is a good rule and the best thing anybody in nine counties has ever made, and in four and a half years not one road has been shut, and the man who said a road might be shut has not shown that it may be shut, and he is not giving a man a name for a rule about a thing that has never happened to anybody.** The badge-man's reason for saying it twice is on the page: **in about nine days a serjeant of a captain of about forty-four is going to arrive somewhere with a sheet and about four men and a question, and about nine people in nine counties are going to be asked which of the two they want, that the second clause is a good rule or that the man with the badge said a road might be shut, and he is not going to let anybody in nine counties be asked that in a room.** **No character resolved whether a clause that cannot be used is the best thing this county has built in four and a half years or the end of it; the badge-man asked the question out loud in a market street and nobody answered it, and the woman of about thirty-six said she had had a month to sit with not knowing and was not going to give it up to make a better afternoon.** The last act is nine men walking out of a market street at the fourth hour and the man of about fifty-two saying at the low end of the boards that his road is not a thing for anybody in that street to write down.

1587. **THE COST OF THE BLOCK, IN FULL, AND IT IS A PERSON, AND THE MANNER IS THE MANUSCRIPT'S.** **A woman of about forty-nine who keeps a kitchen and four beds on the made road, nine years old, lost a fortnight of a hired boy of about fourteen at fourpence a day and two beds of trade out of four, and said it once in a market street in about nine minutes and did not raise her voice once.** The whole of it: **a man of about thirty-one in a livery came to her door on the Friday before last, which is the twentieth of Wolfmonth, and asked her for a witness, and she said no, and he wrote it down, and he read it back to her, and he said that it was very good of her. On the Tuesday after it, which is the twenty-fourth of Wolfmonth, he came back and did not ask her a question, he said he wanted a person who could read a number out loud, because there is a post at the quarry end with a figure on it, and she said the boy of about fourteen could not read, and he said he would find out, and he found out at about the fourth hour of the same afternoon in her own yard, and wrote it down, and read it back, and said it was very good of the boy.
