1623. **THE TWO PEOPLE THIS BLOCK SPENT ITS TWO NAMES ON, AND THE ROSTER OF NEW PEOPLE BY AGE AND TRADE SO A SUCCESSOR DOES NOT DOUBLE THEM.** **`Orme Skell`, forty-one, keeps a drove, a byre and about nine hundred yards of wall at the head of the made road and shuts a five-bar gate with the bar on the lane side.** He is the man of Chapter 0700 who said ten words at a gate. **He has been asked exactly one question about a road in about eleven years. He corrects the county's figure about the gate from about four and a half years to about eleven years and nobody argues with him because it was his gate. He opens the gate for about as long as it takes to shut a cart, on his own account, gives no reason anybody can repeat, and does not go up the lane.** **`Dav Rundle`, nineteen, keeps bees and has kept them since he was about eleven, and is the person who has seen smoke off a chimney behind a wall on that made road four days in seven since he was about eleven and has never asked what is behind it.** He is the only person in nine counties who goes up the lane and he goes on his own business for the broom, three weeks early, and loses about nine frames of brood to a late frost and gets nothing off the shoulder at all. **Also in this block and NOT spends, being established:** the man of about thirty-three who sells cloth three doors up; the man of about thirty-four with a satchel; the copyist of about twenty-six; the badge-man of about fifty-eight; the woman of about thirty-nine who keeps the second gate; the woman of about fifty-one who keeps four beds; the woman of about forty-nine who keeps a kitchen and four beds on that made road; the man of about fifty-two who keeps four miles of made road under contract; the man of about thirty-eight who puts boards on the inside of gates; the man who keeps the weigh-house; the chandler of thirty-three at the low end of the boards, who is Nell Drury and is not named in this block; and **a man of about twenty-seven who keeps eels in weirs two days north of the causeway, and a boy of about eleven who keeps goats on the low side of that lane, and a man of about twenty-six in the market street whose trade nobody knows, and none of those three is named.**

1624. **THE FOUR THINGS, IN FULL, AND THEY ARE SAID ONCE AND ARE NOT A LIST.** Chapter 0707, Thawmonth the nineteenth YR 322, in a market street with about four hundred people in it and about two hundred stopped, the largest crowd in that market town since the twenty-fifth of Hearthmonth. **`Dav Rundle` says four things out loud, in the open, in his own order and not anybody's order, and they are: that the lane is about two miles, is not made, and turns twice, and that past the turns there are about nine hundred yards of road kerbed on both sides and then the kerbs stop; that after the kerbs stop it goes on in the grass as a line about nine feet wide for about nine hundred yards and a person can stand at the end of it and look back and see every yard; that there are nine stones set across it at the end, about the height of a knee, with the tops of them all at the same height; and that at about the ninth hour there was a cart on the other side of the nine stones and he heard it once and it went away and did not come back.** **A man of about twenty-six begins to say them back in order and to number them and gets as far as the second number and about nine people in that street say the same word at the same moment, and he stops, and nobody has ever established whose word it was.** The four things were given out loud and are not written down then or afterwards, and about five people in four hundred say out loud that they believe them, and nobody knows who those five are and they have not spoken to each other.

1625. **THE BLOCK'S COST AND ITS FINDING, IN FULL, AND THE FINDING IS AGAINST THE VOLUME'S OWN DISCOVERY.** **The volume's own discovery turns out to be un-actionable for the first time in about four and a half years, and Mudmonth is why.** The water came up in the lane at the head of the made road on the twenty-fourth of Thawmonth and it has been under since, which is seven days on the first of Mudmonth, and about nine people in nine counties know that the lane goes under about twice a year and about five have ever seen it do it. **A man of about thirty-eight who puts boards on the inside of gates says out loud on the first of Mudmonth that a gate at the end of a lane that is under is not shut and is not open and is the third thing, and that nobody has a name for the third thing.** **The five costs named on the page are a causeway, a drove, nine hundred yards of wall, a walled yard of nine hives, and four miles of made road** and every one of them came out of somebody's trade and not out of anybody's opinion. **The block's other cost is a piece of paper in circulation that is wrong about the protagonist in the direction that makes him more use to somebody, and nobody in nine counties knows who asked for it.**

1626. **THE RELATIONSHIP IS OPEN AND WAS NOT MENDED IN THIS BLOCK.** **There is no exchange of words between Aren Kest and the woman of about twenty-nine who keeps the room in any of the ten files.** She is in no room with him, she did not send for him, nobody sent anybody to her, she was not in the lane and not at the top of the stairs on the days she was absent, and **the thing that joins them in this block is a leaf of paper that came out of her room and into a market street and put a figure on him that is wrong.** **He walks down the four hundred paces of that lane on the first day of a year, stops at about the thirteenth pace at a gutter with about nine days of grit in the ice in it, goes on to her door, does not knock, does not put his hand on it, stands on the step for about as long as it takes to turn a cart, and goes back up the lane.** Nothing between them is in any document and the four and a half years are never referred to as four and a half years about the two of them.

1627. **THE ONE RESOLUTION THE PROTAGONIST MAKES IN THIS BLOCK, IN FULL, AND IT IS THE FIRST THING IN FOUR VOLUMES HE DOES NOT TRY AGAIN.** Chapter 0704: **he does not ask Orme Skell a second question, and he gives the reason out loud in his own trade**, which is that he has asked nine hundred girders the same question twice, and that you do not ask a man who keeps beasts about a road twice, because the first answer is the whole of what he has, and a second question makes it a thing he has been asked to produce, and then it is a thing with a use in it, and he has watched a thing with a use in it come down a road and take a crossing with it. **A successor may not have him ask a second question, may not have the man of forty-one explain himself twice, and may not resolve in a room whether he asked on purpose or worked out on purpose that a man who says ten words at a gate has said all ten on purpose.** That ambiguity is the Volume 14 close's handed-on question and it is still open and is not this block's to close.

1628. **THE TEN MONEY COLUMNS, WITH THE EXTRACTOR FIRST, AND THE COLLISION TEST THIS BLOCK USED.** **The test used is the strongest form available and it is this: each of the ten printed totals was rendered as its spelled phrase and the literal phrase was searched for, as a string, across all seven hundred prior chapter files read whole, and none of the ten phrases occurs in any of them.** **The ten totals are 161, 175, 184, 186, 194, 248, 298, 307, 311 and 332 pence**, ten distinct, ascending, ten items a column, ten distinct prices within a column, the lowest price is 12 and the highest is 40, **no barred numeral (14, 20, 26, 33, 38, 41) appears as a price, an over-figure or a printed total**, and the over-figures are 17, 31, 40, 42, 2, 8, 10, 19, 23 and 44. **EVERY COLUMN FOOTS FOUR WAYS: the printed words parse to the column's own sum, the ten item prices add to that sum, the words printed as the list of the ten prices parse to that same sum, and the marks-and-over clause multiplies back out at forty-eight pence a mark.** **And the ceiling this block found is worth recording because it is arithmetic and not taste: with ten distinct prices drawn from 1 to 41 with the barred six excluded, the largest achievable total is 332, which is 40+37+36+35+34+32+31+30+29+28, so NO column in this manuscript can carry a printed total above three hundred and thirty-two pence while the price band and the barred numerals stand.** A successor that needs a total above 332 has to change the price band on the page and print the change.

1629. **THE MEASUREMENTS OF BLOCK 0001, RECOUNTED ON THE TEN FILES AS THEY STAND, WITH THE TWO BASES NAMED SEPARATELY.** **BASIS ONE, the file total: a plain whitespace split with the title line INCLUDED, each file counted on its own and the ten added. THE BLOCK IS 32,404**, per chapter **3,765 / 3,673 / 3,565 / 3,304 / 3,154 / 3,338 / 2,823 / 2,742 / 3,173 / 2,867**, and a concatenated `cat` of the ten returns 32,404 and every file ends in a newline, so the two bases agree on this measure. **BASIS TWO, the scene body: everything above the paragraph that opens the cost coda, which therefore includes the title line and the dateline, so a body is not a scene body.** The codas, located as everything from the paragraph that opens the cost coda, run 201 to 264 words across the ten. **The mean is 3,240**, against Volume 14's volume mean of 3,449 and its five block means of 3,184, 3,498, 3,324, 3,695 and 3,539, and **so Volume 15 Block 0001 sits 206 words a chapter below Volume 14's volume mean and every one of its ten chapters is inside Volume 14's own range of 2,304 to 3,881.** `about` IS **21.32 to 29.49 per 1,000**, floor at `chapter-0703.md` and ceiling at `chapter-0705.md`, **and TWO of the ten pages stand at or above 28.00, which is `chapter-0705.md` at 29.49 and `chapter-0706.md` at 28.16.** Volume 14's last block ran 23.00 to 27.69 with no page at or above 28.00, **so this block does NOT meet that band and does not claim to, and the reason is the census sentence and not the page.** **Zero em dashes, zero en dashes, zero semicolons, zero exclamation marks, zero single asterisks and therefore no italics, zero straight double quotes and zero curly apostrophes**, curly doubles balanced at 25 in the block, per-line bold parity even on every line, every file ends in a newline, and **1 / 1 / 1 / 1 / 1 / 1 / 2 / 1 / 1 / 1 question marks, eleven in all, with no chapter at zero.** **Exact duplicate paragraphs at twelve words or more are 0 against the seven hundred prior files and 0 inside the block.** **The longest shared eight-word runs against the seven hundred prior files are the census frame and the dateline frame and are house recurrences**: the string *people in nine counties have said since that* occurs 175 times in the block and in every prior block, and *nine people in nine counties have said since* occurs 135 times. **A successor that re-measures should use `.get(window, <empty>)` and never `index[window]`, for the reason at `state/continuity.md` 1530.** **THE THANK FAMILY IS 250 ACROSS THE TEN FILES, BEING 249 USES OF *thanked* AND ONE OF *thank*, AND EVERY ONE OF THE 250 IS A NEGATION OR A PLAIN STATEMENT THAT A PERSON WAS NOT THANKED**, and no Crown officer's formulaic courtesy at a gate is put back in anywhere.

1630. **THE WORDS THIS BLOCK KEPT AT ZERO, AND THE FIGURES AFTER THE BLOCK.** **`stage`, `ledger`, `qualification`, `failed`, `charter`, `custodian`, `mechanism`, `small`, `brother`, `sister`, `Edric` and `Hunn` are each at 0 across all ten files**, and the kin-term is at 0 and the antagonist's name is at 0, both as `outline/volume-15.md` §16 items 4 and 5 require. **THE MANUSCRIPT IS 2,009,934 WORDS IN SEVEN HUNDRED AND TEN CHAPTERS**, recounted file by file on all seven hundred and ten files. **Volumes 01 to 14 are 1,977,530 in seven hundred and did not move by one word**, because nothing outside `chapters/volume-15/`, the state files and `outline/volume-15.md` was touched. **Volume 15 is 32,404 in ten chapters at a mean of 3,240**, and the two volume figures add to the manuscript figure exactly: 1,977,530 + 32,404 = 2,009,934. **AND THE FIGURE 1,977,530 IS THE FIGURE THE VOLUME 14 CLOSE RECORD SET AND IT IS CORRECT AND IT DID NOT MOVE**, and 172,432 is Volume 14's figure and did not move either, and 35,393 is Batch 0005 of Volume 14 and 36,958 is Batch 0004 of Volume 14 and neither moved. **Volume 15 is open and is not closed and no close record for it exists.**

## Volume 15, Batch 0002, the second block of Volume 15, Chapters 711 to 720

1631. **THE BLOCK'S FRAME, DERIVED AND NOT COPIED, AND IT FIXES A MONTH LENGTH THAT WAS LEFT OPEN.** The single anchor is day 2310, the twenty-fifth of Goatmonth YR 321, a Monday, the last page of Volume 14, on the carried thirty-day month, and **Block 0002 is days 2371 to 2430 inclusive, sixty days, every day of it YR 322.** **THE TEN CHAPTER DAYS ARE 2371, 2374, 2406, 2412, 2413, 2416, 2418, 2422, 2424 and 2427,** and their printed datelines are **Mudmonth 26 and Mudmonth 29, and Longlight 1, 7, 8, 11, 13, 17, 19 and 22, all YR 322.** **Five are market mornings and five are not**, at 2371, 2406, 2413, 2418 and 2427, and the five that are not carry the room over the weigh-house shut behind its own door in the dateline on the page. **One is the first of a month, Longlight the first, which is a Saturday market morning.** **The block's last chapter falls on day 2427 and the block's last day is 2430, so days 2428 to 2430 carry no chapter of their own and fall inside Chapter 0720's span; a successor may not move any of the ten days.** **THE PRINTED CALENDAR TABLE AT `outline/volume-13.md` §17 WAS NOT READ FOR ANY DAY OF THIS BLOCK.** **AND MUDMONTH'S LENGTH IS NOW FIXED, AT SIXTY DAYS, AND IT IS FIXED HERE BECAUSE THIS BLOCK NEEDS IT.** Chapter 0709 prints Mudmonth the first as day 2346 and Chapter 0710 prints Mudmonth the twenty-fifth as day 2370, so Mudmonth the first is day 2346; **Longlight the first is day 2406, and that figure is derived twice and independently: Volume 14's own table gives Longlight 1 of YR 321 as day 2046 and a Wednesday, and the year is twelve carried thirty-day months, so Longlight 1 of YR 322 is 2046 + 360 = 2406 and a Saturday, and the block prompt and `outline/volume-15.md` §17 both print 2406.** 2406 minus 2346 is sixty, **so Mudmonth YR 322 runs days 2346 to 2405 and is sixty days long**, which `state/archive/continuity-volumes-01-08.md` item 258 recorded as unfixed and available. **NO CHAPTER OF THIS BLOCK PRINTS A DAY OF MONTH ABOVE THIRTY**, because no chapter is dated in days 2376 to 2405, and **a successor may not date a chapter in that window without printing a day of month above thirty and saying on the page that Mudmonth is sixty days.**

1632. **THE GIFT, IN FULL, AND IT IS A SHOULDER THAT DRIES AND A PARTY THAT IS NOT A LIST.** **Chapter 0713, Longlight the first, a Saturday and a market morning and the first of a month: the shoulder dries, and five people say out loud in a market street, each in their own trade, why they are going up a lane on their own business.** **The first of a month in this county comes whether or not anybody is in the street for it, and the first of Longlight came on a Saturday with about four hundred people in that street and not one of them at the boards.** **The shoulder came up on the nineteenth of Mudmonth and the lane at its foot came out of the water on the twenty-eighth of Mudmonth, and about nine days passed in between in which the ground was firm and there was no way of getting to it.** **The five are:** the man of about fifty-two who keeps four miles of made road under contract for about nineteen years, thirty words; the man of about thirty-eight who puts boards on the inside of gates, fifty words; the man of about twenty-seven who keeps eels in weirs about two days north of the causeway, forty-four words; the woman of about thirty-nine who keeps the second gate from the top of that made road, whose reason is reported and not numbered; and the man of about thirty-four with a satchel, whose reason is that he is the mouth that says the finding of things in this county and has said four of them and has never had one checked. **The man of fifty-eight with a badge and no head says what the party is, in fifty-six words: there are five people going up a lane in this county on their own business and there is no head of it and there is no paper of it, and sending one of them would make a head, and nobody in this street has sent anybody.** **The man of thirty-two is the sixth and is not one of the five, and about four people in that market street counted five and about five counted six and nobody said so out loud.** **And Longlight is the month a boundary oath-stone is renewed in this world, about nine people in nine counties know it, and the man of about thirty-three who sells cloth three doors up says in twenty-four words that there is no oath-stone in this county and no boundary on that made road to renew and that a month is a month, and nobody argues with him and nobody agrees with him.** The badge-man's opening sentence on the month is eight words.

1633. **THE TEST, IN FULL, AND IT IS TWO OF NINE WITH ONE OUTSIDER AND NO FURTHER.** **Chapter 0716, Longlight the eleventh, a Tuesday and not a market morning, five days up on the shoulder: two of the nine households that keep beasts on the flat past the nine stones came up to the line of the stones on the ninth of Longlight, and one outsider came with them, and the other five stayed nine hundred yards back at the end of the line.** **The reason seven and not nine is a rota of beasts that has been kept about nine years and has not been broken once in the nine years: seven of the nine had stock on the flat in the week of the eleventh of Longlight and two did not, and a household with beasts on a flat in the middle of a drying month cannot be off that flat for a morning and cannot do it for a week.** **The man of about fifty-four who keeps the far end of that lane says the reason out loud in his own trade in thirty-nine words: seven of us have the flat this week and two do not, and there is no other reason and there is not going to be one, and anybody who says it is fear or manners is not keeping beasts.** The person of about forty-four whose household keeps sheep says it for herself in forty-five words and the second of the two says it for herself in thirty-one words, and the first of them then says out loud, in thirty words, why the outsider is wanted at a line of stones: **you are not one of us and you have not got a beast on that flat and that is the whole of what I want at a line of stones.** **Nobody crossed the nine stones and nothing on the other side of them was seen by anybody on this side of them.**

1634. **THE ONE NAME THIS BLOCK SPENT, AND IT IS SPENT ONCE.** **`Tarr Lyster`, about fifty-four, keeps the far end of that lane, which is to say he keeps the boundary of the common the nine households share, and he looks at that boundary about four times a year and about nine years.** He is named once in Chapter 0716, in the narration, in the form the house uses for `Dav Rundle`, **and the name is printed once in the block and the other two references to him in that chapter are not by name.** **He is not one of the nine households and he is not counted among the nine and he is not a head and he has no paper.** He is the man who stands about nine feet off the line of the nine stones with his back to about nine hundred yards of flat ground and looks at the tops of them, and who went back to the boundary on the thirteenth of Longlight and said nothing at all about a crossing. **The volume's six-name budget therefore stands at THREE of six spent** and **three slots remain for Blocks 0003 to 0005.** `Iven` and `Marrow` were both rejected as the name because both are established names in earlier volumes and a recombination of them would read as a return.

1635. **THE HEARING OF THE FAR SIDE, IN FULL, AND IT IS HEARD ONCE AND ONCE ONLY.** **Chapter 0717, Longlight the thirteenth, a Thursday and a market morning four days off in that market town with no market on the made road, the ninth hour on a clear day, nine people on the shoulder.** **The sound is three strokes of a thing being struck on something hard, and it came from about four hundred yards off and went to about nine hundred yards off in about as long as it takes to turn a cart, and it did not come again.** **It is not the cart of the twelfth of Thawmonth and the two are not the same sound.** **The man of about thirty-four with a satchel says it out loud, in the open, in his own trade, in fifty-five words: a thing heard once is not a road and it is not a place and it is not a fact anybody can build on, and he says things out loud in a market street and has said them for four and a half years and nobody has ever built anything on one of them. Nobody agreed with him and nobody argued with him and he said nothing else that day.** **No chapter of this block shows anything on the other side of the nine stones, and no chapter has a cart appear a second time as a fact, and the only description of the far side anywhere in the block is twenty-three words long and is in Chapter 0714: the ground goes on flat and low, there is a fence line, and there are beasts on the other side of it.**

1636. **THE COST, IN FULL, AND IT IS A CROSSING THAT IS NOT THERE, SAID BY A PERSON WITH NO STANDING TO SAY IT.** **Chapter 0718, Longlight the seventeenth, a Monday and not a market morning, eleven days on the shoulder, nine people on it.** **The man of thirty-two says it out loud, in the open, at about the eleventh hour, once, and it is fifty-four words: there is no crossing at these nine stones and there is not going to be one from this side, and the whole of what this party came up here to do cannot be done from this side, and I have got no standing in this county to say so and I am saying it. Nobody argued with him and nobody thanked him.** **The person of about forty-four whose household keeps sheep says the other half out loud, in her own trade: the crossing is not what nine households are for and there is no rota for it and nobody in nine counties would ever agree to it.** **The five trades are then counted out on the shoulder by the five themselves, and four figures are given: four miles of made road a month short of a contractor, nine yards of boards not going on gates for about a fortnight, a gate left and not shut, and thirteen days of a mouth that says things not saying them, and the fifth man refuses in the open to give his own cost and nobody asks him.** The party came down on the eighteenth of Longlight and went five ways and gave no account of anything to anybody.

1637. **THE BLOCK'S CLOSE, IN FULL, AND IT IS TWO TRADES IN ONE MARKET STREET.** **Chapter 0720, Longlight the twenty-second, a Saturday and a market morning, about four hundred people and about two hundred stopped, the room open.** **The man of about fifty-eight with a badge and no head says the true thing about what a hearing is worth, in forty-seven words: a hearing is worth the paper it makes and this one has made nothing in four and a half years, and what it made is worth what the Marshal's hand makes of it, which is nothing, and that is the whole of what a hearing is worth.** **The man of about thirty-three who sells cloth three doors up answers in his own trade, in sixty-six words: four hundred who have heard are not in the same place as four hundred who have not, and a person who has heard a thing can be found and a person who has not heard a thing cannot be found, and there is no other difference between the two of them and there never was.** **The badge-man answers in forty-seven words that four hundred who have heard cannot be asked, and the reason has not changed since the first of Goatmonth, and it is that asking is making a head, and nobody has ever asked him what a head is for.** **They disagree in the open for about as long as it takes to turn a cart and nobody agrees with either of them and nobody takes a side and nobody thanks anybody.** **THE QUESTION THE PROTAGONIST CANNOT ASK IS ON THE LAST PAGE AND IT IS WHETHER FOUR HUNDRED PEOPLE WHO HAVE HEARD THE SAME FOUR THINGS ARE IN A DIFFERENT POSITION FROM FOUR HUNDRED PEOPLE WHO HAVE NOT. He worked it out at about the tenth hour of that morning at the sea end of that street and he did not say it out loud, and the reason he did not is that asking it would be sending, and a man who asks a market street whether four hundred people are a thing has made four hundred people into a thing.** **About four people in nine counties think the whole of this county's business is finished and about five think it is not, and neither set has ever spoken to the other.**

1638. **THE RELATIONSHIP IS OPEN AND WAS NOT MENDED, AND ONE NEW BEAT IS ON THE PAGE.** **There is no exchange of words between Aren Kest and the woman of about twenty-nine who keeps the room in any of the ten files, and nothing between them goes in any document.** **Chapter 0711, on a market morning: he goes up the thirteen stairs for the first time anybody in nine counties has seen him in that room in about four and a half years, and there are two people in the room, and neither of them says anything to him and he says nothing to either of them, and he stands in it about as long as it takes to turn a cart, looks at a press standing open with no date on it, and comes down.** **The copyist of about twenty-six is in the room and the woman who keeps it is in the room and he does not go up that lane again in this block, on the nineteenth of Longlight he comes in at the eighth hour and the room is shut behind its own door and he does not go up the lane.** The register gains no entry in this block and the day-book stands at eleven entries and the fourth ruled head is empty and no fifth head is ruled. **The four and a half years are never described as four and a half years about the two of them.** The sentence that a paper which makes a man older makes him more use to somebody is repeated once, in Chapter 0720, in a market street, and nothing else about the age is moved.

1639. **THE TEN MONEY COLUMNS, WITH THE EXTRACTOR FIRST, AND THE COLLISION TEST THIS BLOCK USED.** **The extractor is the printed total, clause-anchored on `is <spelled number> pence`, at a parsed value of sixty or above, taken over all seven hundred and ten prior chapter files read whole and not sliced to the last two paragraphs, and a second extractor on the restated total inside `and <spelled number> pence is <spelled number> marks and <spelled number> over`.** **Extractor one: 770 instances, 284 distinct. Extractor two: 266 distinct. Union distinct: 290.** **The ten totals are 152, 153, 165, 167, 171, 282, 309, 315, 327 and 330 pence**, ten distinct, ascending, ten items a column, ten distinct prices within a column, **the lowest price in the block is 3 and the highest is 40**, **no barred numeral (14, 20, 26, 33, 38, 41) appears as a price, an over-figure or a printed total anywhere in the block's money**, and the over-figures are 8, 9, 21, 23, 27, 42, 21, 27, 39 and 42. **EVERY COLUMN FOOTS FOUR WAYS: the printed words parse to the column's own sum, the ten item prices add to that sum, the words printed as the list of the ten prices parse to that same sum, and the marks-and-over clause multiplies back out at forty-eight pence a mark.** **COLLISION RESULT: ZERO.** **And the arithmetic ceiling recorded at 1628 stands and holds: no column in this manuscript can carry a printed total above three hundred and thirty-two pence while the price band and the barred numerals stand.**

1640. **SUPERSEDED BY 1656 AND 1666. THE MEASUREMENTS OF BLOCK 0002 AS THEY STOOD WHEN IT WAS FIRST WRITTEN. A SUCCESSOR WHO READS ONLY THIS ITEM WILL INHERIT 33,985 AND THE PER-CHAPTER FIGURES BELOW, AND BOTH HAVE MOVED TWICE: the first repair pass at 1652 and the second at 1666. THE STANDING FIGURES ARE 34,009 AND THE TEN PER-CHAPTER FIGURES 3,365 / 3,383 / 3,569 / 3,597 / 3,441 / 3,414 / 3,466 / 3,327 / 3,134 / 3,313, WITH THE MINIMUM 3,134 AT `chapter-0719.md` AND THE MAXIMUM 3,597 AT `chapter-0714.md`. NOTHING ELSE IN VOLUME 15 MOVED AND NO DATELINE, PRICE, TOTAL OR SPEECH WAS TOUCHED BY EITHER PASS.** **THE MEASUREMENTS OF BLOCK 0002, RECOUNTED ON THE TEN FILES AS THEY STAND, WITH THE TWO BASES NAMED SEPARATELY.** **BASIS ONE, the file total: a plain whitespace split with the title line INCLUDED, each file counted on its own and the ten added. THE BLOCK IS 33,985**, per chapter **3,356 / 3,383 / 3,571 / 3,597 / 3,430 / 3,414 / 3,469 / 3,327 / 3,125 / 3,313**, and every file ends in a newline. **The minimum in the block is 3,125 at `chapter-0719.md` and the maximum is 3,597 at `chapter-0714.md`, and no chapter of this block is below three thousand words.** **BASIS TWO, the scene body: everything above the paragraph that opens the cost coda, which therefore includes the title line and the dateline, so a body is not a scene body.** The bodies run 3,128 / 3,153 / 3,321 / 3,364 / 3,187 / 3,179 / 3,226 / 3,065 / 2,871 / 3,075, minimum 2,871 at `chapter-0719.md`. The codas run 228 / 230 / 250 / 233 / 243 / 235 / 243 / 262 / 254 / 238, summing to 2,416, which is **7.11** per cent of the block. **THE MEAN IS 3,398**, against Volume 15 Batch 0001's mean of 3,240 and Volume 14's volume mean of 3,449, **so this block is 157 words a chapter above the first block of this volume and 52 below Volume 14's volume mean, and it is the highest Volume 15 block mean so far.** `about` IS **22.26 to 27.67 per 1,000**, floor at `chapter-0716.md` and ceiling at `chapter-0717.md`, **and NO PAGE IS AT OR ABOVE 28.00**, which is the standing advice at `state/continuity.md` 1596 finding twenty-one and against Batch 0001's 21.32 to 29.49 with two pages at or above it. **Zero em dashes, zero en dashes, zero semicolons, zero exclamation marks, zero single asterisks and therefore no italics, zero straight double quotes and zero curly apostrophes**, curly doubles balanced in every file at 2 / 2 / 7 / 4 / 1 / 5 / 3 / 2 / 1 / 4, per-line bold parity even on every line, every file ends in a newline, and **1 / 1 / 1 / 1 / 1 / 1 / 2 / 1 / 1 / 1 question marks, eleven in all, with no chapter at zero.** **Exact duplicate paragraphs at twelve words or more are 0 against the seven hundred and ten prior files and 0 inside the block**, and **five near-duplicates found in a first pass were rewritten in the files and are recorded here because the finding is the useful part: the middle-of-a-morning paragraph, the cloth-on-a-bench paragraph, the market-street paragraph, the weigh-house paragraph and the line that opens the stones recurred and each was re-cut.** **The longest shared eight-word runs against the seven hundred and ten prior files are the census frame, the dateline frame and the cost-coda opening, which are house recurrences**, and there are 1,507 of them at the eight-word window. **A successor that re-measures should use `.get(window, <empty>)` and never `index[window]`, for the reason at `state/continuity.md` 1530.**

1641. **THE WORDS THIS BLOCK KEPT AT ZERO, THE THANK FAMILY, THE NAMES, AND THE FIGURES AFTER THE BLOCK.** **`stage`, `ledger`, `qualification`, `failed`, `charter`, `custodian`, `mechanism`, `small`, `brother`, `sister`, `Edric` and `Hunn` are each at 0 across all ten files**, and the kin-term is at 0 and the antagonist's name is at 0. **`the Marshal's hand` is printed once in the block, in Chapter 0720, and the volume's cap of two printings of the antagonist's name is untouched and both are still held for Blocks 0003 and 0005.** **THE THANK FAMILY IS 213 ACROSS THE TEN FILES, BEING 211 USES OF *thanked* AND TWO OF *thank*, AND EVERY ONE OF THE 213 IS A NEGATION OR A PLAIN STATEMENT THAT A PERSON WAS NOT THANKE.** No Crown officer's formulaic courtesy at a gate is put back in anywhere. **A capitalised two-word sweep over the ten files returns only `Tarr Lyster`, once, and `Nine Elms`, once, and both are accounted for.** **THE MANUSCRIPT IS 2,043,919 WORDS IN SEVEN HUNDRED AND TWENTY CHAPTERS**, recounted file by file on all seven hundred and twenty files. **Volumes 01 to 14 are 1,977,530 in seven hundred and did not move by one word**, because nothing outside `chapters/volume-15/` and the state files was touched. **Volume 15 is 66,389 in twenty chapters, being 32,404 for Batch 0001 and 33,985 for Batch 0002, and the column foots: 1,977,530 + 32,404 + 33,985 = 2,043,919.** **The figures 1,977,530, 172,432 and 32,404 are the figures a successor may inherit without recounting, and 2,043,919 is the manuscript figure after this block and must be recounted, not inherited.**

1642. **THE BLOCK'S FRAME, DERIVED AND NOT COPIED, AND THE TEN DAYS.** The single anchor is day 2310, the twenty-fifth of Goatmonth YR 321, a Monday, on the carried thirty-day month, and the chain is built forward from it. **Block 0003 is days 2431 to 2490 inclusive, sixty days, and every day of it is YR 322.** Longlight the first is day 2406 and is carried at thirty days, so **Longlight 30 is day 2435 and Rainmonth the first is day 2436, a Monday and not a market morning, and Rainmonth is carried at thirty days, so Rainmonth 30 is day 2465 and Harvestmonth the first is day 2466, a Wednesday and not a market morning.** **The block crosses TWO month boundaries and not one, at day 2436 and at day 2466, and an earlier wording of this item said one and named only day 2436, which contradicted this same item's own sentence about two of the ten days being firsts of a month; that wording is withdrawn.** **The ten chapter days are 2432, 2434, 2435, 2436, 2439, 2443, 2448, 2452, 2466 and 2490**, and their printed datelines are **Longlight 27, Longlight 29, Longlight 30, Rainmonth 1, Rainmonth 4, Rainmonth 8, Rainmonth 13, Rainmonth 17, Harvestmonth 1 and Harvestmonth 25, all YR 322.** **Five are market mornings and five are not**, at 2432, 2434, 2439, 2448 and 2490, and the five that are not carry the room over the weigh-house shut behind its own door in the dateline on the page. **Two are the first of a month, Rainmonth the first and Harvestmonth the first, and neither is a market morning.** **The last of the ten is the block's last day, day 2490, and a successor may not move one of the ten days.** The printed weekday table at `outline/volume-13.md` §17 was not read for any day of this block. **And Hearthmonth is the eleventh month of the year and not the third**: `state/continuity.md` 1580 gives the first of Hearthmonth YR 321 as day 2256 and it is a Wednesday, which agrees with the chain, so the first of Hearthmonth YR 322 is day 2616 and it falls six days after the last day of Volume 15, and **the phrase *before Hearthmonth* is a future reference in this volume and not a past one.** The month between Mudmonth and Longlight that the carried thirty-day chain requires, days 2376 to 2405, is `Greenmonth`, which is the third month of the year and is absorbed into Mudmonth YR 322's sixty days.

1643. **THE GIFT, IN FULL, AND IT IS THE BROOM.** **Chapter 0725, Rainmonth the fourth, YR 322, a Thursday and a market morning.** The broom on that east rise is about nine acres and comes on about the fourth of Rainmonth every year and is the only broom within a day's walk of a walled yard of nine hives, and about nine people in nine counties know that and about five have ever cut it. **The young man of about nineteen said it out loud, in the open, to about nine people, in his own trade, and it was thirty-nine words: the broom is on the east rise this morning and I am going up at six for it and I am going on my own account and I am not waiting for anybody and there is nothing to arrange.** Nobody said anything back, nobody thanked him and nobody sent anybody with him. **This is the first time in about four and a half years that anybody in this county has said out loud in a market street that they are going up that lane for the thing the lane is actually for, and the thing is a broom.** He went up with the drove, which goes out every Thursday, because a drove is the only errand in this county that already goes up that lane, and the drove came back down at about the fifth hour of that Thursday and he did not go with it, and **a man of about thirty-two with a stick went up with it on the same morning and did not go with it either.** Asked out loud in that market street, kindly, what he would do with the rest of it, he answered in one speech of fifty-six words that he will cut what four bundles of twine will hold, tie the rest in nine bundles and leave them on the stones at the top for whoever wants them, and that there has been one bundle left there every year since he was eleven and it is never the same man, and in Chapter 0730 he said out loud, in his own trade, and it was fifty-four words, **how to tell whether a man has cut a bundle or taken it: the twine cut off in nine places and the stalk end split, and that nobody has ever left one whole.** **Nobody crossed the nine stones and nothing on the other side of them was seen by anybody on this side of them, and the broom was not a reason to cross and is not made one anywhere in the block.**

1644. **THE FINDING, IN FULL, AND IT IS SMALL AND IT IS ABOUT KEEPING. AND IT IS THE NINE HOUSEHOLDS AND THEIR ROTA AND NOT TWO HOUSEHOLDS, WHICH IS A REVISION OF AN EARLER DRAFT OF THIS ITEM AND IS THE BINDING VERSION.** **Chapter 0726, Rainmonth the eighth, four days on the shoulder, and Chapter 0727, Rainmonth the thirteenth, in a market street, and the two of them are not the same three things said in the same order both times: 0726 gives the extent first and 0727 gives a checkable location first, and the second of 0727's three is a shortening of 0726's second, and the third of 0726 IS THE CANONICAL TEXT OF THE FINDING AND THE THIRD OF 0727 IS A DIFFERENT SENTENCE OF SEVENTY-SIX WORDS, WHICH IS ITEM 1650'S RULING AND WHICH GOVERNS EVERY PLACE A SUCCESSOR QUOTES THE THIRD FINDING. An earlier wording of this item called the third of the two the same seventy-eight words said twice, and that wording is withdrawn.** **The young man of about nineteen says them in his own order and not anybody's order.** **One, what is on the flat, sixty-nine words on the shoulder and forty-nine in the street: there is about nine hundred yards of kerbed road on this shoulder and then about nine hundred yards of a line nine feet wide in the grass and then nine stones at the end of it, and I walked all of it in Thawmonth and I walked all of it again this week and it is where I said it was and it is not nine yards different anywhere; and, in the street, there is nine hundred yards of kerbed road on that shoulder and the kerb stops where the grass comes over it, and I can put my hand on the stone where it stops and nine people in nine counties can check it in two days if anybody wants to. Second, how long it has been going, seventy-two words on the shoulder and fifty-seven in the street: the line is nine feet wide because the east rise is cut along it once a year and a line in grass that is not cut closes over in about three years, and I have seen two other nine-foot lines in this county and one of them was gone in two years and one of them was gone in five, and that is how I know it is being kept and not left; and, in the street, the line is nine feet wide because the east rise is cut along it once a year and a line in grass that is not cut closes over in about three years, and I have seen two other lines like it in this county and one was gone in two years and one was gone in five. Third, who keeps it, SEVENTY-EIGHT WORDS ON THE SHOULDER AND SEVENTY-SIX IN THE STREET AND NOT THE SAME SENTENCE: the nine households past those nine stones keep a rota of that flat that has been kept about nine years and has not been broken once in the nine years, and the grass on the road is cut with the flat and not as a road, and nobody else cuts any of it, and the kerb is older than the cutting and I cannot tell you how much older because there is nobody in nine counties to ask.** **The ninth-foot line is a line that is cut, and the cutting is the keeping, and about four people in nine counties have said since that a road is nine feet wide because a man cuts the grass on it once a year, and about four people have said since that nine households have been keeping nine hundred yards of an old road going for about nine years without a head on it and without a paper and that it is the longest anything anybody in nine counties has ever heard of being kept.** The man of thirty-two asked one question out loud on that shoulder at about the eighth hour of the fourth day, **what is it for**, and the answer was thirty-four words in the young man's own trade: **it is not for anything, it is a boundary first and it is nine hundred yards of it, and whatever it was before that nobody in nine counties knows and nobody has asked me.** **And the second half of the finding is twelve words, said out loud on that shoulder at about the eighth hour of the seventh day, and they are: a thing that is found is not a thing that is used.** **The ninth thing anybody in nine counties has said out loud about that lane was said in a market street on the thirteenth of Rainmonth by the same young man, and the first of the three of them was a place where the kerb stops, and about four people in that street had worked out inside about nine seconds that it answered a question asked in that street on the twenty-ninth of Longlight and about five had not.** The man of about thirty-four with a satchel said out loud in that street, in his own trade, and it was fifty-seven words, **that it is a young man's account and not his, that he has said nine things out loud in this county in about four and a half years, and that he is not going to say the tenth and is not going to say this one twice.**

1645. **THE TWO ACCOUNTS OF THAT ROAD WERE PUT IN FRONT OF EACH OTHER AND THEY DO NOT AGREE, IN FULL.** **Chapter 0721, Longlight the twenty-seventh, a Thursday and a market morning.** The man of about thirty-three who sells cloth three doors up took the blue wool off his own forearm, which is the second time in the volume after Chapter 0702, and said it out loud, in the open, at the low end of those boards, in his own trade, and it was fifty-five words: **a road that goes between two places has both of the places under the grass, and I have sold cloth into nine counties for nine years and nobody has ever come into my shop and asked me where either of them is. That is what a road is. It is a thing between two things.** **About an hour later, out of a different trade and not in answer, the man of about fifty-two who keeps four miles of made road under contract for about nineteen years put his hand flat on nine feet of a new kerb standing against a shop wall and said sixty-six words: a road that goes across a shoulder and does not go off it is a road that goes to a place that is not there any more, and I have laid stone on the low side of a hill for about nineteen years and I can tell you the two ends of a road that goes to a place, and both of mine are still standing.** The cloth seller answered in forty-four words, **that then it is not a road, it is a lane, and a lane that goes nowhere is the oldest thing in nine counties and I have been selling cloth beside it for about nine years and it has never once asked me for anything**, and the roadkeeper said one word back and the word was no. **Neither of those two men said where the kerb stops, and the man of about thirty-four with a satchel said so out loud, in his own trade, in forty-seven words: nobody in nine counties can say where the kerb stops, one person has stood where it stops and has not written it down and has not said it, and the rest of us have been about four and a half years getting used to not having it.** **And in Chapter 0730, on the twenty-fifth of Harvestmonth, the same two trades say out loud what about four and a half years is worth and get two answers: the roadkeeper, seventy-eight words, that it has produced about nine hundred yards of a road on a hilltop and a hearing that has made nothing, and that he mends nine yards of wall in about a hundred and forty-three days because a wall has to be mended, and that he has got no trade at all for nine hundred yards of a road and that is not a roadkeeper's job; and the cloth seller, eighty-one words, that it has produced about four hundred people who have heard nine things and nobody anybody could find about any of them, and that he can find a person who has heard a thing in about nine seconds and has never once found one who has not heard it, and that a finding in four hundred mouths is worth four hundred shops and three clauses in nine hands with no copy are worth nothing.** Nobody agreed with either of them and nobody thanked anybody. **About four people in nine counties think the whole of this county's business is finished and about five think it is not, and neither set has ever spoken to the other, and a broom and about nine hundred yards of an old road have not changed the size of either set.** **THE TWO WORD COUNTS AT THE END OF THIS ITEM ARE SEVENTY-EIGHT AND EIGHTY-ONE AND NOT SEVENTY-SIX AND SEVENTY-NINE, AND THE EARLIER FIGURES ARE CORRECTED BY A REPAIR PASS THAT RE-COUNTED THE PRINTED SPEECHES IN `chapter-0730.md` WORD BY WORD. The cloth seller takes the blue wool off a third time in Chapter 0730 and the chapter says three times, and the second time is Chapter 0721 and that chapter says twice, and both are correct because Chapter 0702 was the first.**

1646. **THE MIDPOINT REVERSAL, IN FULL, AND IT IS ONE PARAGRAPH AND NOBODY NOTICES IT.** **Chapter 0726, on the fourth day on that shoulder, at about the fourth hour.** The left palm of the man of thirty-two was warm, and it was the warmth it has about four days in seven since the first of Embermonth, and there was a thing at it that would have taken him to about the ninth hour of a night he cannot put a year to, and he stood on that shoulder with his hand open and looked at the ground at his own feet for about as long as it takes to turn a cart. **It was a transfer and he witnessed it and did not conduct it, and there is no other set of words for a thing like that in nine counties, and nobody on that shoulder used them and nobody anywhere has asked him for any.** The young man of about nineteen was about nine hundred yards off up the east rise cutting broom and did not look up. **He told nobody that afternoon or the next morning or on the way down, and about as long as it takes to turn a cart after the fourth hour he cut about nine inches off a stalk of broom with the sickle the young man had left lying on a stone and put it inside his coat, and nobody asked him what he was doing and he did not say.** **No character in this block asks him about it, no character notices the warmth of the left palm, no System panel is printed, no route is opened or closed or cut, the Many-Handed is not crossed, the notebook's separate leaf that says *a person* is not looked at, the mechanism is not explained, and no chapter says what a roadmark is.** **Nobody in nine counties knows it happened, including the man with the satchel, including the woman who keeps the room, and nobody has asked him and nobody is going to until Block 0004.**

1647. **THE THING THE BLOCK DID NOT DO, IN ONE LIST, AND IT IS ADDITIONS TO 637 AND TO EVERY CLAUSE OF 630 AND OF 624 THAT IS STILL TRUE.** **Nobody crossed the nine stones in this block and nothing on the other side of them was seen by anybody on this side of them.** **No cart appeared a second time as a fact and no sound was heard out of that direction again, and the whole of what this volume has about the far side is unchanged: three strokes heard once on the thirteenth of Longlight, one cart heard once on the twelfth of Thawmonth, and twenty-three words of description.** **Nine households did not walk the stones in this block: no third household came up the lane between the eighth of Rainmonth and the seventeenth and nobody asked it for a reason, and the reason would have been the rota of beasts kept about nine years.** **The protagonist's age is not re-opened; thirty-two stands and the sentence that a paper which makes a man older makes him more use to somebody is not printed in this block at all.** **The antagonist's name is printed ZERO times in this block.** `Edric Hunn` is at 0 across all ten files, **the volume's cap of two printings is untouched, and the one reference in this block is *the Marshal's hand*, printed once, in Chapter 0720, which is Block 0002's file and not this block's.** `outline/volume-15.md` §16 item 4 allocates one of the two printings to Block 0003, and `workspace/volume-15/batch-0003/PROMPT.md` permits at most one in this block and only if a document requires it; **no document in this block required it, so zero were printed and both printings stand available to Block 0005, which may use both, and a successor should read this as a reconciliation and not as a cap that has been spent.** **The kin-term is at 0.** **No System panel is printed and the volume's one is still reserved for Block 0005.** **The words *stage*, *ledger*, *qualification*, *failed*, *charter*, *custodian*, *mechanism* and *small* are each at 0 across all ten files, and so are *mother*, *father*, *son*, *daughter*, *brother* and *sister*.** **The mechanism is not explained, the roadmark is not looked at, the separate leaf is not looked at, and no cut is made.** **The cloth over the bench is not lifted and nobody asks what is under it.** **No head is filled or ruled, no finding is put under any of the ten questions, the half leaf is not turned or timed, the bar at the Low Gate is not lifted or scheduled, the fourth course of stone is not laid or scheduled, and the mallet is not picked up, and the three courses of sill, the line of stone and the mallet are not in this block at all.** **The two written refusals of Volume 14 were not turned into a body, a practice, a rule or a head, the second leaf was not read out, nobody demanded it, and it was not addressed to the protagonist, and the nine households are not a body and were not made one.** **The separation was not mended: there is no exchange of words between Aren Kest and the woman of about twenty-nine who keeps the room in any of the ten files, nothing between them goes in any document, the register gains no entry, the day-book stands at eleven entries, the fourth ruled head is empty and no fifth head is ruled, and the four and a half years are never described as four and a half years about the two of them.** **The protagonist is not asked a second question about the gate at the head of the made road, the man of about forty-one does not explain himself twice, and it is not resolved in a room or on a road whether the first question went unasked on purpose.** **Nobody traced who asked the fen county road office for the roll.** **None of the four holes in the arrangement is called small, none is resolved, none is renumbered and no fifth was opened.** **The road that went quiet in Thawmonth YR 319 was not got back, named, visited or sent to.** **The carter's mate of about twenty-two was not found, the coast's twenty-second notch was not cut, the shut road four hundred miles off was not reopened, and nobody was sent to the city four hundred miles off.** **The three series are three series, the band figure was not printed, and no number went in the drawer.** **No path's open-crossing count was converted into main-world days and no path mouth was monthed, weekdayed or tided.** **The column did not come back up the made road, the last twenty-one counties were not read off the list, the captain did not apologise and was not called a knave or a fool.** **Nobody was thanked anywhere in this block.** **No new final enemy was introduced and no Volume 16 was created.**

1648. **THE ORDINARY HALF OF THE BLOCK, IN FIGURES, BECAUSE A SUCCESSOR NEEDS THEM, AND THREE OF THESE FIGURES ARE CORRECTED BY A REPAIR PASS THAT WENT BACK TO THE FILES WORD BY WORD.** **Nine yards of wall came down on the twenty-eighth of Thawmonth and about six of the nine are back in on the twenty-seventh of Longlight and still six on the seventeenth of Rainmonth, about seven of the nine are back in on the first of Harvestmonth with a gap of about two yards, and about eight of the nine are back in on the twenty-fifth of Harvestmonth with a gap of about a yard, and the gap is about four feet high throughout, and the man of about forty-one has carried stone about two miles on his own since the second of Mudmonth, which is a hundred and forty-three days at the twenty-fifth of Harvestmonth. AN EARLIER WORDING OF THIS ITEM SAID ABOUT SEVEN OF THE NINE BACK IN AND A GAP OF ABOUT TWO YARDS AT THE CLOSE OF THE BLOCK, AND BOTH FIGURES ARE WRONG: `chapter-0730.md` prints eight of the nine yards back in and the gap about a yard long, and the seven and the two yards belong to `chapter-0729.md`, which is the first of Harvestmonth and nine days earlier.** **The bar at the head of that made road went in on the lane side on the Thursday morning after the water went out of the lane and it is in and it has not been asked about.** **The drove has gone up that lane about nine hundred yards on every Thursday since the second of Mudmonth, which is thirteen Thursdays by Longlight the thirtieth, fifteen by Rainmonth the seventeenth, seventeen by the first of Harvestmonth and TWENTY-ONE by the twenty-fifth, and it stood in the field with the stone wall round it and came back at about the fifth hour, and nobody went with it, and it goes out on a Thursday and not on any other day and has not gone out on a Sunday in about eleven years. AN EARLIER WORDING OF THIS ITEM SAID ABOUT TWENTY-FIVE BY THE TWENTY-FIFTH AND THAT IS WRONG: the first Thursday on or after the second of Mudmonth is day 2350 and the twenty-first is day 2490 itself, so the count on the twenty-fifth of Harvestmonth is twenty-one and not twenty-five.** **The hives stand at three frames of brood where there were nine on the night before the eighth of Thawmonth, and a brood frame comes back in about four weeks in a year like this one, and the count of days is footed from that night in every chapter that prints it: a hundred and nine days at 0721, a hundred and eleven at 0722, a hundred and twelve at 0723, a hundred and thirteen at 0724, a hundred and sixteen at 0725, a hundred and twenty-nine at 0728, a hundred and forty-three at 0729 and a hundred and sixty-seven at the close of the block, and all eight reproduce on the chain because the night before the eighth of Thawmonth is the night of the seventh, which ends on day 2323.** **About nine people took a broom or a bee brush off a board at the low end of that market street on the thirteenth of Rainmonth and about five did not, and there is a broom standing in the house of the man of about forty-one and he did not buy it and nobody has said that he did and nobody has said that he did not.** **The hearing has not been called since the first of Goatmonth, cannot be called, and does not sit, and its firsts came and gone seven times running with nothing at the boards, at 2406, 2436 and 2466 in this block and four before it, and the badge-man has not been at two of them on purpose.** **About four hundred and nine parish rolls come past the floor of that weigh-house in a good month and Rainmonth is the month about half of them come past wet and Harvestmonth is the month they come past in their best state.**

1649. **THE TEN MONEY COLUMNS, WITH THE EXTRACTOR FIRST, AND THE COLLISION TEST THIS BLOCK USED.** **The extractor is the printed total, clause-anchored on `is <spelled number> pence`, at a parsed value of sixty or above, taken over all seven hundred and twenty prior chapter files read whole and not sliced to the last two paragraphs, and a second extractor on the restated total inside `and <spelled number> pence is <spelled number> marks and <spelled number> over`, and hyphens and the compounds `twopence` and `fourpence` were normalised before parsing, which is what makes the instance and distinct counts agree with the figure at 1639.** **Extractor one: 335 instances, 269 distinct, over seven hundred and twenty prior files read whole. The union of the anchored and the unanchored form is 284 distinct, which is the figure at 1639, and the twenty-two values in the band sixty to three hundred and thirty-two that neither extractor reaches are 61, 66, 84, 132, 136, 166, 212, 218, 230, 240, 254, 266, 273, 281, 310, 314, 321, 325, 326, 328 and 329. AND THE STRONGEST TEST AVAILABLE WAS ALSO RUN, WHICH IS THE ONE AT 1628: each of this block's ten printed totals was rendered as its spelled phrase and the literal phrase was searched for across all seven hundred and twenty prior files read whole, together with its no-*and* form and its digit form, and the twenty-nine values that pass that test and also carry a non-barred over-figure are 66, 100, 109, 111, 113, 114, 115, 118, 121, 124, 126, 127, 131, 133, 135, 136, 140, 141, 143, 145, 146, 149, 150, 155, 163, 166, 169, 310 and 328.** **The ten totals are 66, 100, 113, 126, 140, 155, 163, 169, 310 and 328 pence**, ten distinct, ascending, ten items a column, ten distinct item names a column, ten distinct prices a column, **the lowest price in the block is 1 and the highest is 40**, **no barred numeral (14, 20, 26, 33, 38, 41) appears as a price, an over-figure or a printed total anywhere in the block's money**, and the over-figures are 18, 4, 17, 30, 44, 11, 19, 25, 22 and 40. **TEN DISTINCT TAILS: the last two items and their prices are (barrow wheel 9, hurdle rail 21), (hurdle rail 24, cresset 40), (coping stone 36, cresset 40), (saw blade 37, barrow 40), (hurdle stile 37, barrow 40), (hand adze 39, cresset 40), (barrow wheel 39, cresset 40), (hinge 39, cresset 40), (saw blade 39, barrow 40) and (whetstone 39, barrow 40), and no two of the ten are the same pair.** **EVERY COLUMN FOOTS FOUR WAYS: the printed words parse to the column's own sum, the ten item prices add to that sum, the words printed as the list of the ten prices parse to that same sum, and the marks-and-over clause multiplies back out at forty-eight pence a mark. The conversion was checked separately from the sum.** **COLLISION RESULT: ZERO UNDER THE INSTRUMENT AS THIS ITEM DEFINES IT, AND THAT WORDING IS NOW QUALIFIED, AND A SUCCESSOR MUST READ THIS PARAGRAPH BEFORE IT CHOOSES TEN TOTALS FOR ITS OWN TEN COLUMNS.** **THE ZERO IS TRUE OF THE LITERAL-PHRASE TEST AT 1628 AND IT IS NOT TRUE OF A TEST THAT READS BOTH SPELLINGS.** **What was re-run in the repair pass: each of this block's ten printed totals was rendered as its spelled phrase and searched across all seven hundred and twenty prior files read whole in EVERY spelling the manuscript uses, which is two per total above one hundred because this corpus prints both *one hundred* and *a hundred*, and in the digit form as well. THE RESULT IS SEVEN OF THE TEN ALREADY PRINTED AS A TOTAL IN THIS CORPUS IN THE OLDER SPELLING: *a hundred pence* twice, *a hundred and thirteen pence* twice, *a hundred and twenty-six pence* twice, *a hundred and forty pence* twice, *a hundred and fifty-five pence* twice, *a hundred and sixty-three pence* twice and *a hundred and sixty-nine pence* once, all of them in Volumes 09 and 10. The three that are printed nowhere in either spelling are 66, 310 and 328.** **THE REPAIR DID NOT RE-CUT THE SEVEN COLUMNS, AND THE REASON IS ARITHMETIC AND NOT PREFERENCE. A spelled search over the same seven hundred and twenty files, in both spellings and for every value in the band sixty to three hundred and thirty-two, leaves EIGHTEEN free values out of the TWO HUNDRED AND SEVENTY-THREE in that band, and they are 66, 132, 136, 166, 212, 218, 230, 254, 266, 273, 281, 308, 310, 314, 321, 326, 328 and 329. Twelve of those eighteen carry an over-figure that is one of the barred six, which leaves SIX, and they are 66, 132, 136, 166, 310 and 328.** **A block needs ten columns and there are six usable free totals in the whole manuscript, so TEN COLLISION-FREE TOTALS CANNOT BE WRITTEN IN THIS BAND BY ANY BLOCK THAT FOLLOWS, AND A SUCCESSOR LOOKING FOR A BETTER SET IS LOOKING FOR SOMETHING THAT DOES NOT EXIST.** **A SUCCESSOR SHOULD THEREFORE DO ONE OF TWO THINGS AND SAY ON THE PAGE WHICH ONE IT DID: note that the free space is six values and reuse a value knowingly, which is a fact about the manuscript and not a failure, or carry a total below sixty, which sits outside the collision set entirely because the extractor is anchored at sixty, and that is the cleaner of the two because it does not repeat a figure anybody has printed.** **AND THE FIGURE 335 INSTANCES AND 269 DISTINCT FOR EXTRACTOR ONE IS NOT REPRODUCED BY THE REPAIR PASS, WHICH RAN THE EXTRACTOR AS DEFINED AND RETURNED A SMALLER COUNT, because the count depends on where the extractor is trimmed and this item does not say where the trim is. THE FIGURE IS THEREFORE NOT RE-ASSERTED AS RE-MEASURED AND A SUCCESSOR SHOULD PRINT ITS OWN COUNT WITH ITS OWN TRIM. The free-set list printed earlier in this item says twenty-two and lists twenty-one, so the count and the list disagree and neither can be relied on without the two-spelling search above.** **And the arithmetic ceiling recorded at 1628 stands and holds and was re-checked in the repair pass: with ten distinct prices drawn from 1 to 41 with the barred six excluded, the largest achievable total is 332, so no column in this manuscript can carry a printed total above three hundred and thirty-two pence while the price band and the barred numerals stand, and the block's highest is 328.**

1650. **THE MEASUREMENTS OF BLOCK 0003, RECOUNTED ON THE TEN FILES AS THEY STAND AFTER A FULL REVIEW AND REPAIR PASS, WITH THE TWO BASES NAMED SEPARATELY.** **BASIS ONE, the file total: a plain whitespace split with the title line INCLUDED, each file counted on its own and the ten added. THE BLOCK IS 34,707**, per chapter **3,329 / 3,423 / 3,469 / 3,424 / 3,438 / 3,836 / 3,364 / 3,498 / 3,401 / 3,524**, and every file ends in a newline. **The minimum in the block is 3,329 at `chapter-0721.md` and the maximum is 3,836 at `chapter-0726.md`, which is the shoulder chapter and which the repair pass added about three hundred words to at about the fourth hour of the fourth day, and no chapter of this block is below three thousand words.** **BASIS TWO, the scene body: everything above the paragraph that opens the cost coda, which therefore includes the title line and the dateline, so a body is not a scene body. The bodies ARE 3,109 / 3,213 / 3,251 / 3,184 / 3,203 / 3,581 / 3,137 / 3,255 / 3,157 / 3,227, minimum 3,109 at `chapter-0721.md`. The codas ARE 220 / 210 / 218 / 240 / 235 / 255 / 228 / 243 / 244 / 297, summing to 2,390, which is 6.89 per cent of the block.** **THE MEAN IS 3,470, WHICH IS 34,707 OVER TEN WITH THE REMAINDER THROWN AWAY, and a successor who rounds instead of truncating gets 3,471 and both figures are this same block**, against Volume 15 Batch 0002's mean of 3,398 and Batch 0001's mean of 3,240, and Volume 14's volume mean of 3,449, **so this block is seventy-two words a chapter above Batch 0002, two hundred and thirty above Batch 0001 and twenty-one above Volume 14's volume mean, and it is the third Volume 15 block clear of the standing prose debt and the first of the three to stand above Volume 14's volume mean.** `about` IS **24.99 to 26.59 per 1,000**, floor at `chapter-0729.md` and ceiling at `chapter-0728.md`, **and the numerator is CASE-SENSITIVE and not case-insensitive, over the FILE TOTAL and not the body, which is the only combination of the two that reproduces this range, and a successor who counts `About` and `ABOUT` as well gets a different ceiling and will conclude the figure here is wrong when the figure here is right**, **and NO PAGE IS AT OR ABOVE 28.00**, which is the standing advice at `state/continuity.md` 1596 finding twenty-one, and every one of the ten pages sits inside the twenty-two to twenty-seven aim. **Zero em dashes, zero en dashes, zero semicolons, zero exclamation marks, zero single asterisks and therefore no italics, zero straight double quotes and zero curly apostrophes**, curly doubles balanced in every file at 6 / 2 / 2 / 3 / 4 / 6 / 8 / 2 / 4 / 5, per-line bold parity even on every line, every file ends in a newline, and **ONE QUESTION MARK IN EVERY FILE, ten in all, with no chapter at zero.** **A capitalised two-word sweep over the ten files returns NOTHING AT ALL**, which is the cleanest name result this volume has produced and is the reason the block spends no name. **THE NAME BUDGET FOR VOLUME 15 THEREFORE STANDS AT THREE OF SIX SPENT WITH THREE SLOTS UNSPENT**, and `outline/volume-15.md` §8's line that Block 3 spends a third name is satisfied by Block 0002's `Tarr Lyster` and is reconciled here, and a successor may spend up to three names in Blocks 0004 and 0005. ****EXACT DUPLICATE PARAGRAPHS AT TWELVE WORDS OR MORE ARE NOW ZERO IN VOLUME 15, AND THE TWO THAT STOOD IN THIS BLOCK ON ITS FIRST PASS HAVE BEEN REPAIRED, AND THE EARLIER WORDING OF THIS ITEM IS SUPERSEDED BY IT.** **The first was the market-morning opener of `chapter-0722.md`, which was byte-identical to line 5 of `chapter-0711.md` in Batch 0002, and it is now its own paragraph, still a market-morning frame, with one new fact in it: on this one the four hundred and the two hundred did not want an about in front of them, because a man standing at the low end of those boards can count the ones who stop and get the same number a second time. The frame is a house device and it recurs; the frame is not a copy.** **The second was the young man's third finding, which stood at line 35 of `chapter-0726.md` and at line 49 of `chapter-0727.md` as the same seventy-eight words on the shoulder on the eighth of Rainmonth and in the market street on the thirteenth. It is now seventy-six words in `chapter-0727.md` and it is a different sentence: he has watched nine households cut that flat for nine years from the top of it and is the only one who has watched it from the top and not from a gate, and the cutting is not for the road, it is for beasts, and the kerb was under the grass before the first of them ever cut, and that is the whole reason nobody has ever put a hand on that road. The shoulder version in `chapter-0726.md` is untouched and is still the canonical text of the finding, and every place in the state files that quotes the finding quotes the shoulder version.** **THE EARLIER WORDING OF THIS ITEM CALLED THAT REPETITION DELIBERATE AND LOAD-BEARING, AND THAT RULING IS REVERSED. In a manuscript whose whole device is dating utterances and counting their words, the same seventy-eight words said twice in two settings five days apart is a continuity defect and not a motif, and the item's own reason for the ruling was that a person says a thing out loud in a trade and then says it out loud in a street, which the chapter can now do without the words being the same, because a man retelling a finding in a street in front of two hundred people says it differently and slower, and the new sentence is the better one and it is also the one that explains why nobody asked a second question. `AGENTS.md` puts no duplicated paragraphs in the completion gate, and a device is not an exemption from the gate.** **NOTE FOR A SUCCESSOR, BECAUSE IT CORRECTS A FIGURE AT ITEM 1640 AND A FIGURE AT THE END OF THIS ITEM: the manuscript as a whole does contain exact duplicate paragraphs of twelve words or more, and the reproducible figure over all seven hundred and thirty files is FORTY-NINE GROUPS AND FIFTY-SIX SURPLUS OCCURRENCES, which is fifty-six paragraphs that repeat one already on disk. There are four counts a successor might quote and they are not interchangeable: forty-nine groups, fifty-six surplus occurrences, eleven groups if the italic document lines are excluded, and forty-six of the forty-nine if the two files of a pair must be different files, which leaves three that repeat inside a single file. No variant of the instrument gives the thirty-seven that an earlier pass recorded here, and that figure is withdrawn.** **A measurement of the first fourteen words of every paragraph of twenty-five words or more finds no paragraph inside this block that repeats another paragraph inside this block, and finds forty that share an opening with a paragraph in the seven hundred and twenty prior files, and every one of the forty is the census frame or the dateline frame and is a house recurrence and not a duplicate.** A measurement of the first fourteen words of every paragraph of twenty-five words or more finds no paragraph inside this block that repeats another paragraph inside this block, and finds forty that share an opening with a paragraph in the seven hundred and twenty prior files, and every one of the forty is the census frame or the dateline frame and is a house recurrence and not a duplicate.** **A successor that re-measures should use `.get(window, <empty>)` and never `index[window]`, for the reason at `state/continuity.md` 1530.**

1651. **THE FIGURES AFTER BLOCK 0003, THE THANK FAMILY, AND WHAT A SUCCESSOR MAY INHERIT.** **`stage`, `ledger`, `qualification`, `failed`, `charter`, `custodian`, `mechanism`, `small`, `mother`, `father`, `son`, `daughter`, `brother`, `sister`, `Edric` and `Hunn` are each at 0 across all ten files, the kin-term is at 0 and the antagonist's name is at 0, and *the Marshal's hand* is printed once in the block, in Chapter 0720, which is Batch 0002's file.** **THE THANK FAMILY IS 167 ACROSS THE TEN FILES, being 167 uses of *thanked* and no use of the bare *thank*, and every one of the 167 is a negation or a plain statement that a person was not thanked**, and no Crown officer's formulaic courtesy at a gate is put back in anywhere. **THE MANUSCRIPT IS 2,078,626 WORDS IN SEVEN HUNDRED AND THIRTY CHAPTERS**, recounted file by file on all seven hundred and thirty files, and this figure and the two beneath it moved inside this block's own repair pass and not when the block was first written. **Volumes 01 to 14 are 1,977,530 in seven hundred and did not move by one word**, because nothing outside `chapters/volume-15/` and the state files was touched. **Volume 15 is 101,096 in thirty chapters, being 32,404 for Batch 0001, 33,985 for Batch 0002 and 34,707 for Batch 0003, and the column foots: 1,977,530 + 32,404 + 33,985 + 34,707 = 2,078,626.** **The four figures 1,977,530, 172,432, 32,404 and 33,985 are the figures a successor may inherit without recounting. The figures 2,078,626 for the manuscript, 101,096 for the volume and 34,707 for the block must be recounted and not inherited, together with the block mean 3,470 and the volume mean 3,369, and both means are truncations with the remainder thrown away, so a successor who rounds gets 3,471 and 3,370 and both are the same chapters.** **And the protagonist's age may not be recounted or re-opened, because it is resolved on the page at 1620 and in Chapter 0708 and thirty-two stands.** **And the calendar may be inherited from 1642 and re-derived, and the printed table at `outline/volume-13.md` §17 remains a certificate and not an authority.**

1652. **THE FIRST REPAIR PASS ON VOLUME 15 BATCH 0002, IN FULL, AND ITS SIZE IS THIRTY-TWO CHANGED LINES ACROSS TEN OF THE TEN FILES AND NO CHAPTER WAS REWRITTEN.** **This block was written in an earlier run, was reviewed against the whole of its own brief, and was then repaired on the page. No chapter of 711 to 720 was rewritten and no paragraph of scene prose was replaced.** **THE NET MOVEMENT OF THE FIRST PASS WAS ONE HUNDRED AND FORTY-SEVEN WORDS DOWN, FROM 33,985 TO 33,838, AND AN EARLIER WORDING OF THIS ITEM SAID ONE HUNDRED AND TWENTY-THREE AND SAID THAT EVERY WORD OF IT WAS IN `chapter-0715.md` AND `chapter-0719.md`, AND BOTH HALVES OF THAT ARE WRONG. The measured per-file movement is plus nine at `chapter-0711.md`, minus two at `chapter-0713.md`, minus one hundred and sixty at `chapter-0715.md`, minus three at `chapter-0717.md` and plus nine at `chapter-0719.md`, and 0712, 0714, 0716, 0718 and 0720 did not move by one word, so the movement is in FIVE FILES AND NOT TWO.** **The figure a successor may quote for this pass is therefore MINUS ONE HUNDRED AND FORTY-SEVEN, and the thirty-two changed lines are thirty-two added and thirty-two removed in `git diff c9dd1a8^ c9dd1a8 --numstat -- chapters/`, one changed line counting once, and the count of EDITS and the count of CHANGED LINES are different measures because a single correction that appears in three places in one file is one edit and three changed lines. The item's own list below names twenty corrections and the second round at 1659 closed five more, so the correction count is twenty-five and it is not the same measure as either number above.** The edits, in file order: **`chapter-0711.md`**, *first*, a retrospective that pinned itself to the chapter's own dateline, and the word *Saturday* became *market morning*, because a Mudmonth 26 Saturday cannot narrate a Sunday night and a Monday morning and then say Saturday; *this one was itself defective and was corrected at 1662, because the Monday it narrates is not a market morning in this county either*; *second*, the wall that came down on the twenty-eighth of Thawmonth had been described as down for about thirty days on the twenty-sixth of Mudmonth, and it is twenty-eight, and the figure now foots with the thirty-one days `chapter-0712.md` prints from the twenty-ninth, and it appears in three places in the file including the cost coda; *third*, the window on the shoulder was nine days from the nineteenth to the twenty-eighth of Mudmonth in a chapter dated the twenty-sixth, so seven of those days had run, and every figure in the file now reads seven, INCLUDING THE THIRTY-SIX-WORD SPEECH, WHICH STILL IS THIRTY-SIX WORDS, and the coda names seven; *fourth and fifth*, the printed question, *then has anybody walked on it*, dropped the scope its own answer needed, because the answer was that nobody had, and that denies the young man of about nineteen who stood on that shoulder on the twelfth of Thawmonth and is recorded ten paragraphs earlier in the same file, and the question now reads *then has anybody in nine counties walked on it since the nineteenth of Mudmonth*, which is fourteen words and carries no count.

1653. **THE SEVEN REMAINING EDITS, IN FILE ORDER, AND EACH ONE IS THE FINDING CLASS THIS MANUSCRIPT'S REVIEWS KEEP NAMING.** **`chapter-0712.md`**, one frame of brood was nine frames *on the night before the eighth of Thawmonth*, which is day 2322, where every other file in the volume including this one's own later arithmetic says the night of the eighth, and the frost came on that night, and the phrase is now the night of the eighth. **`chapter-0713.md`**, *first*, the lamp at the high end of the boards was not lit at the fourth hour on page one and was lit at the fourth hour on page two of the same chapter, on a Saturday, and the standing rule is that it is lit at the fourth hour every day of the year, and the exception is gone; *second*, the fourth of Rainmonth was fourteen days off on the first of Longlight and it is thirty-three, and the twenty-seven days off that `chapter-0714.md` prints six days later now foots with it; *third*, the refusal of the nineteenth of Thawmonth was called the fifth time when `chapter-0709.md` calls the badge-man's the first, `chapter-0715.md` calls the cloth seller's the second and the next the third, and `chapter-0720.md` calls it the third made by two people in two trades, and it is now the first, and the sentence was made to parse; *fourth*, the cost coda put the man of about thirty-eight who puts boards on the inside of gates into that count as the third refusal, and he refuses a third thing about gates and not the list of nine names, and he is now outside the count. **`chapter-0714.md`**, *first*, the causeway lamp is put there every Monday afternoon in the winter and was not put there on a Wednesday, in a chapter dated a Friday, and it is now a Friday; *second*, the block's only description of the far side of the nine stones was said to be nine words long and it is twenty-three, and the coda in the same file already printed twenty-three, and `state/continuity.md` 1635 already records twenty-three, and the figure is now twenty-three in both places. **`chapter-0716.md`**, the second of the two households was counted twice in one chapter, and it is thirty-one words by the speech and by the coda and it read nine in two places, and both are now thirty-one. **`chapter-0717.md`**, *first*, the satchel man's one sentence about the sound was said to be eleven words and it is thirteen, and this is the class of finding this manuscript's reviews name most often, that a figure attached to a printed speech is footed off the printed words beside it; *second*, a census sentence said *anybody in this block* has put a sound on that shoulder, and the word *block* is production language standing in a character's mouth, the same class that took Volume 15 to zero for the word *volume*, and the sentence now reads that a sound has been put on that shoulder.

1654. **THE LAST FOUR EDITS, IN FILE ORDER.** **`chapter-0718.md`**, the fourth of Rainmonth was about fourteen days off on the seventeenth of Longlight and it is seventeen, and it now foots with the twenty-one days between the thirteenth and the fourth that `chapter-0717.md` prints. **`chapter-0719.md`**, *first*, the frost frame was a day out and read the night before the eighth of Thawmonth; *second*, a paragraph put four hundred people and a Saturday market into a chapter that says on its own dateline and in its own body that it is a Wednesday with no market in the street and nine people in it, and a second paragraph carried a seventeen-day interval that `chapter-0720.md` already carries correctly, and both paragraphs were deleted, and the satchel man is left going south into the market town on that Wednesday, which is what the surviving line at the head of that chapter already said he did, and the chapter was then restored above three thousand words with two new paragraphs of scene prose, one of which covers the room over the weigh-house shut behind its own door, which its dateline promised and its body had not delivered, and none of which is a correction of anybody's account. **`chapter-0720.md`**, the six people came down two miles of unmade road *three days ago* from a chapter dated the twenty-second of Longlight, and they came down on the eighteenth, and it is four days. **And one verbatim run of shared prose was re-cut, and the record of it was WRONG IN ITS ARITHMETIC AND IS CORRECTED HERE. AN EARLIER WORDING OF THIS ITEM SAID THAT TWO EXACT DUPLICATE PARAGRAPHS WERE RE-CUT, FIFTY-TWO WORDS SHARED VERBATIM BETWEEN `chapter-0711.md` AND `chapter-0715.md` AND SIXTY WORDS SHARED BETWEEN `chapter-0702.md` AND `chapter-0715.md, AND NEITHER HALF OF THAT REPRODUCES. There was ONE run, not two, and it was one hundred and four words, and THREE files carried it and not two, being `chapter-0702.md`, `chapter-0711.md` and `chapter-0715.md`, and measuring the longest common word-run between 0715 and 0711 and between 0715 and 0702 returns the same hundred and four words both times because it is the same run counted twice. The three paragraphs differed at the tail and NO WHOLE PARAGRAPH OF TWELVE WORDS OR MORE WAS EVER BYTE-IDENTICAL ACROSS THEM, which is why the whole-paragraph instrument counted Volume 15 as zero before the repair as well as after it.
